Start Here

New to After World? Read this section first. It is the front of the Core System, and it explains the game in plain English.

What is After World?

After World is a tabletop role-playing game. A small group of friends sit around a table (or on a video call) and tell a story together. There is no board and no winner. The story is the point.

  • One person is the Game Master, or GM. The GM describes the world, plays the villains, monsters and everyone else you meet, and decides what happens.
  • Everyone else is a player. Each player controls one hero, called a player character or PC.
  • You say what your hero does. When the result is uncertain, you roll dice. The dice and the rules decide whether it works.

There are no classes. You build your hero from a race (or species), a background (what you did before the adventure), six attributes and some traits (special skills and abilities). That means two heroes of the same race can still be very different.

After World can be played in a fantasy world, a modern world or a Star Wars galaxy. The Core System has the rules every game uses. A Theme adds the setting.

The golden rule. After World is built on trust and fun. The book says that everything is a guideline, not a rule. If your group wants to change something, talk about it and change it.

What you need

You need Details
People One GM and one or more players. The GM tools in this book are tuned for a party of about four players.
Dice One ten-sided die (d10) and one each of d4, d6, d8 and d12. One set of dice can be shared around the table, but a set each is faster.
Paper and pencil For notes and your character sheet.
A character sheet Use the separate After World character sheet.
This book Everyone should have access to it, on screen or printed.

Which part of the book do I read?

I want to… Read

How the game works

A game is called a session. It usually lasts a few hours. A set of sessions with the same heroes and story is a campaign.

Every session follows the same simple loop:

  1. The GM describes the scene and what the heroes can do. (‘You are on a forest road. A wagon has crashed into a ditch, and you hear voices in the trees.’)
  2. The players say what they want to do. (‘I creep towards the voices.’ ‘I check the wagon.’ ‘I shout a warning.’)
  3. The GM says what happens. Sometimes the answer is simply ‘yes’. When the result is uncertain or risky, the GM asks for a roll.

Then it starts again with a new scene. You can try almost anything you can think of outside combat, such as talking to people, investigating, stocking up on supplies or sneaking about.

When to roll. Roll when there is a real chance of failure and failing would matter. If a task is easy or nothing is at stake, the GM can just let it happen.

Your dice

You use two kinds of dice in After World.

Die What it does
Main Die A ten-sided die (d10). You roll it on every roll. It also decides criticals.
Skill Die A die you add to the Main Die. Which one you roll depends on your Rank in the attribute you are using.

Each attribute has a Rank from 0 to 4. The higher your Rank, the bigger your Skill Die:

Rank Skill Die
0 None. You roll only the Main Die.
1 d4
2 d6
3 d8
4 d12

There are six attributes. When the GM asks for a roll, you use the attribute that fits what you are trying to do:

Attribute Use it for
Brawn Strength. Lifting, pushing, breaking, and hitting hard with heavy weapons.
Agility Speed and nimbleness. Dodging, climbing, sneaking, picking pockets, and fighting with light weapons. Also sets your Combat Defence.
Health Staying conscious and alive. Resisting poison, disease and exhaustion. Also sets your Hit Points.
Smarts Knowledge, memory and clever crafting, tinkering or brewing.
Senses Sight, smell, hearing, touch and taste. Spotting things, aiming a bow, and casting some magic.
Presence Talking, performing and force of personality. Also used for some magic.

Making a roll

This is the most important thing to learn. Every roll works the same way.

  1. Say what you want to do. The GM tells you which attribute to use and what you must beat.
  2. Roll the Main Die (d10). Look at it first:
    • A 10 is a critical success. You succeed straight away, whatever else is on the dice.
    • A 1 is a critical failure. You fail straight away.
  3. Roll your Skill Die (if your Rank is 1 or more) and add it to the Main Die.
  4. Add any bonuses. Traits and tools give bonuses. For example, a tool set you are trained in gives +3 to the jobs it is made for.
  5. Compare your total with the target. If you beat it, you succeed.

What is the target?

Kind of roll The target is…
Attack roll (hitting something) The target’s Combat Defence (CD).
Everything else (climbing, spotting, resisting poison, persuading, picking a lock) A Difficulty Score (DS) chosen by the GM.

The GM decides how hard a task is. If you want a starting point, use this guide. It matches the numbers used elsewhere in the Core System.

Difficulty DS
Easy 8
Standard 10
Hard 12
Heroic 14

Advantage and Disadvantage

The GM can make a task easier or harder by giving you Advantage or Disadvantage.

  • Advantage: roll your dice a second time and keep the higher result.
  • Disadvantage: roll your dice a second time and keep the lower result.

For example, climbing a tree in the rain with no gear would be Disadvantage. Climbing it with proper climbing equipment could be Advantage. If you are not trained in a tool or task, you roll with Disadvantage.

Ties. The book does not say what happens when your total exactly equals the target. A simple table rule is that a tie counts as a success. Agree on this before you start and use it every time.

Worked examples

Here are three short examples with real numbers.

Example 1: a skill roll

Mira is a scout with Senses Rank 3, so her Skill Die is a d8. She presses her ear to a door to hear what is behind it. The GM says it is a Standard task, DS 10.

Mira rolls the Main Die: 6. Not a 1 or a 10, so she carries on. She rolls her d8: 5. Her total is 6 + 5 = 11. That beats DS 10, so she hears two people whispering about a hidden key.

With Advantage, she would have rolled both dice twice and kept the better pair. If the Main Die had been a 10, she would have succeeded straight away. If it had been a 1, she would have failed, and the GM would describe a fitting consequence, such as her bumping the door and being heard.

Example 2: an attack

Brogan is a Kolskon knight. He has Brawn Rank 4 (a d12), an arming sword that does 5 damage, and a total bonus of +2 from his race and a trait. He swings at a bandit: CD 13, Armour Rating 1, 8 HP.

  1. Main Die: 8. Skill Die (d12): 4. Bonus: +2. Total 14. That beats CD 13, so he hits.
  2. Damage: 5 (sword) + 2 (bonuses) = 7.
  3. The bandit’s armour takes 1 off: 6 damage. The bandit is left with 2 HP.

Next turn Brogan rolls a 10 on the Main Die. That is a critical hit: it hits automatically and the damage is doubled, so 7 × 2 = 14. The bandit is down.

Example 3: being hit

The second bandit attacks Brogan. Enemies roll a d10 and add their attack bonus. This bandit has an attack bonus of +8. It rolls 5, so the total is 5 + 8 = 13. Brogan’s Combat Defence is 12, so the attack hits.

The bandit’s sword does 5 damage. Brogan’s studded leather has an Armour Rating of 2, so he takes 5 − 2 = 3 damage. His HP drops from 23 to 20.

Fighting

When a fight starts, the game moves to rounds. In each round, everyone gets one turn. The fight ends when one side is beaten, runs away or surrenders.

Who goes first? The game leaves turn order up to your group on purpose. Decide together what works best for you and use it for the fight. For example, draw cards from a deck and guess higher or lower, or roll a die and pick odd or even. Or simply go around the table.

What you can do on your turn

On your turn you can use all of these once:

Action What it is
Main action Attack or cast a spell.
Movement Move anywhere within your zone, or to another zone.
Interaction Use or equip an item, or say something short. It also lets you make an extra attack with a Light weapon.
Opportunity Action An attack or spell on someone else’s turn, if the GM allows it. Leaving an enemy’s space gives that enemy one.
Surprise Action Any of the above while the enemy cannot see you.

Zones

The game does not use squares or measuring tapes. It uses zones.

Zone What it means
Melee Zone Right next to you. You can hit things here with a melee weapon.
Ranged Zone Further away. Only ranged weapons and spells reach here.
Outer Zone The edge of the map. Too far for most attacks.

You can move around freely inside a zone. Your Speed is how many zones you can cross with your Movement: Small and Medium characters move 1 zone, and Large characters move 2. Heavy armour has Bulk, which reduces your Speed by half a zone for each point. Full rules for zones.

Damage and armour

When an attack beats the target’s Combat Defence:

  1. Work out the damage. Use the weapon’s damage plus any bonuses. A critical hit doubles it.
  2. Subtract the target’s Armour Rating (AR).
  3. But a hit always does at least half its damage, rounded up, however much armour the target has.
  4. Take the result off the target’s Hit Points (HP).

Armour Rating from all your gear together can never be more than 8.

Hit Points, Wounds and dying

  • HP is your health. It is your Health Rank × 5, so 5, 10, 15 or 20. The Healthy trait gives you more.
  • Wounds. You can take a Wound to turn a hit into 0 damage. The cost is that you lose 1 rank from every attribute for the first Wound, 2 for the second, and 3 for the third. You can have 3 Wounds at most. A critical hit against you can also give you a Wound.
  • 0 HP. When you reach 0 HP you are down and start making Death Rolls. On your turn, roll a d6. Above 3 is a pass, below 3 is a fail, and a 3 is rolled again. Roll three times. If you get more passes, you get back up with 1 HP. If you get more fails, you die. If someone attacks you while you are down, you instantly take 2 Wounds.

See Death and Survival for the exact rules.

Resting

Rest Time What it does
Light rest 1 hour Restores magic points equal to your Senses or Presence Rank × 10, and removes most effects.
Middle rest 4 hours A light rest, plus HP equal to your Health score.
Full rest 8 hours A middle rest, plus healing of 2 injuries.

Wounds do not heal with rest alone. They need a potion or surgery, then a full rest. You must sleep 8 hours a day, or you gain Fatigue. See Rests and Fatigue.

Conditions

Many things can happen to you in a fight, such as being Stunned, Grappled or Frightened. The Effects table explains each one in a line or two.

Magic in a nutshell

Magic is described in full in the Fantasy Theme. In short:

  • If you choose magic, you start with 100 magic points and 2 spells at level 1. You can swap a spell for a weapon. You gain 50 more points at every level.
  • Casting a spell is a Main action. Each spell says what it costs.
  • Your Magic DS is 10 + your Senses or Presence Rank. Your magic attack uses your Senses or Presence dice.
  • Spells are Angelic (use Senses), Demonic (use Presence) or Universal (either). See Magic.

Make a hero in ten minutes

You can build a hero in these steps. Follow them in order. The Core System has a longer walk-through at Character Creation.

  1. Have an idea. A tough knight? A sneaky thief? A healer? Pick something you would enjoy playing.
  2. Choose a race. It gives you a starting trait, a bonus rank in one attribute, a special perk, languages and a size. See Races (or the Modern or Star Wars equivalent).
  3. Choose a background. It gives you the tools and weapons you are trained in and a kit of starting gear. See Backgrounds.
  4. Spend 10 ranks on your attributes. Every attribute starts at 0, and none can go above Rank 4. Put your highest ranks into what your hero does most. Your race then gives you its bonus rank on top, so you get 10 ranks plus whatever your race gives you.
  5. Work out your numbers (see the table below).
  6. Choose equipment. You get 2 weapons (or a power, such as a spell) and 1 set of armour. Your background adds a tool set and kit.
  7. Choose a trait. You get your racial trait or traits, plus one more of your choice. Traits are listed in the Ability Database. Where to find the traits.
  8. Fill in your character sheet and show it to your GM.

The numbers you need

Number How to work it out
HP Health Rank × 5: Rank 1 = 5, Rank 2 = 10, Rank 3 = 15, Rank 4 = 20. With the Healthy trait: 9, 16, 23 or 32, plus 1 more every level.
Combat Defence (CD) 12 + your Agility Rank − your armour’s Armour Penalty. With no armour: Rank 1 = 13, Rank 2 = 14, Rank 3 = 15, Rank 4 = 16.
Armour Rating (AR) From your armour. See the table below.
Carry weight Brawn Rank × 5. Light items weigh 1, Medium 3 and Heavy 6.
Speed 1 zone for Small and Medium, 2 for Large, less any armour Bulk × 0.5.
Attack roll Main Die + the Skill Die of the weapon’s attribute + bonuses.
Armour type Armour Rating Armour Penalty Bulk
None (cloth, robes) 0 0 0
Light (leather) 1 −1 0
Reinforced (studded leather) 2 −2 0
Balanced (chain shirt) 3 −2 1
Heavy (half plate) 4 −3 1
Full (full plate) 5 −3 2

Levelling up

At each new level you get 1 rank to spend on any attribute (up to Rank 4) and 1 new trait. If you use magic, you also gain 50 magic points. The GM may also award traits for great moments in the story.

Running your first session

If you are the GM, this is for you. You do not need to know every rule. You need to be fair, keep things moving and make the story fun.

Before you start

  1. Pick a Theme. Fantasy, Modern or Star Wars. Everyone should read its first chapter.
  2. Chat about tone. Is it a serious war story or a light adventure? Decide how dangerous it will be and what everyone is comfortable with.
  3. Help the players make heroes, or give them the ready-made ones.
  4. Write a simple adventure. You need only a place, a problem, some people and a few surprises. There is a ready-made one below.

During the game

  • Describe, then ask. Paint the scene in a few sentences and end with ‘What do you do?’
  • Call for rolls only when it matters. Choose the attribute, set a DS from the guide above, and use Advantage or Disadvantage for anything that helps or hinders.
  • Be generous with creativity. The game is designed to give players as much freedom as possible. If an idea is clever, let it help.
  • Keep fights fast. Use zones, not a map. Enemies roll d10 + their attack bonus against a hero’s Combat Defence. A natural 10 always hits for double damage and a natural 1 always misses.
  • Give consequences, not roadblocks. When a roll fails, something should change in the story, not just ‘nothing happens’.

Building a fair fight

The Enemy Compendium gives you tested enemies. Threat Budget shows how many to use. The enemy table is at The Generalist Line. For a first fight with a party of four, use four Level 1 bandits for a fair fight, or three for a gentler one.

Level 1 bandit (Solo)
HP 8
Combat Defence 13
Armour Rating 1
Attack bonus +8 (roll d10 + 8)
Damage per hit 5

A first adventure: The Broken Bridge

For four level 1 heroes. About two hours.

The hook. A merchant named Orsa Vell hires the heroes to guard her wagon on the road to the town of Highmere. She will pay 100 coins each when they arrive.

Scene 1: The road. The heroes meet Orsa and set out. Let them talk, ask questions and shop for supplies. A good Senses roll (DS 10) spots fresh wagon tracks and a lost boot at the roadside.

Scene 2: The broken bridge. The bridge over a stream has been cut. The water is shallow but cold and fast. The heroes can cross with a Brawn or Agility roll (DS 10), build a ramp with a Smarts roll (DS 10), or find another way across with a Senses roll (DS 12), which takes longer. Failing a roll should cost time or supplies, not end the adventure.

Scene 3: The ambush. Bandits are watching from the trees on the far side. Use three or four bandits (see the stat block above). The bandits are not eager to die. If half of them fall, the rest try to flee and drop something useful. Use zones: two bandits in the ranged zone with bows, the rest in melee.

Scene 4: The truth. A captured bandit (or a clue on one) reveals that Orsa’s rival, Garrick Moss, paid them to wreck the wagon. The heroes must decide what to do. They can confront Moss in Highmere, tell the guards, or take his money and go.

Rewards. Give each hero a share of the coins and an item from Loot, levels 1 to 2. After the adventure, the heroes can level up: each gains 1 rank and 1 trait.

Levelling up. The Core System does not set a schedule. A simple method is to level the party up after each finished adventure or big goal.

If something goes wrong

  • A hero is down. That is normal. Death Rolls give them a fair chance, and their friends can help. Let the group find a way to save them.
  • The fight is too hard. Let the bandits flee, give the heroes a way out, or reduce the enemies’ number. The Compendium’s Breaking Off rule explains why enemies should retreat.
  • A player wants something the rules do not cover. Pick a DS, choose an attribute, roll, and keep playing. You can look the rule up later.

Frequently asked questions

Do I need every book? No. You need the Core System and one Theme. Add a Feature Module, such as Creatures of the Night, or the Enemy Compendium, only if you want them.

What if I do not own all the dice? Use a free dice-rolling app, or share dice around the table. The most-used dice are the d10 and d6.

Is it a problem if we get a rule wrong? No. The book says that everything is a guideline. Pick a ruling, write it down and keep going.

What happens if two books disagree? The more specific rule wins: Core System first, then a Theme, then a Feature Module, then your GM’s house rules.

Can I play with only two or three people? Yes. Use fewer enemies. Give the heroes extra care: a healer and a fighter make a good pair.

Where are the traits and spells? In the Ability Database. Each Theme lists the ones it uses and tells you where to find them.

What is the difference between HP and Wounds? HP is the damage you can take before you fall. A Wound is a more serious injury that you can choose to take instead of damage. It makes you weaker until you recover from it.

Glossary

Word Meaning
Advantage Roll again and keep the higher result.
Armour Rating (AR) A number taken off the damage of every hit against you.
Attribute One of six scores: Brawn, Agility, Health, Smarts, Senses, Presence.
Background What your hero did before the adventure. It gives tools, training and kit.
Bulk How heavy and clumsy armour is. It slows you down.
Campaign A long story told over many sessions.
Combat Defence (CD) The number an attack must beat to hit you.
Critical A natural 10 (automatic success, double damage on an attack) or a natural 1 (automatic failure). See Criticals.
d10, d4, d6, d8, d12 Dice with that many sides.
Death Rolls The d6 rolls you make when you reach 0 HP.
Difficulty Score (DS) The number a non-attack roll must beat, chosen by the GM.
Disadvantage Roll again and keep the lower result.
Fatigue Tiredness points that make you weaker. Level 6 is death. See Fatigue.
GM (Game Master) The person who runs the world, the villains and the story.
HP (Hit Points) How much damage you can take before you fall.
Interaction A small action in your turn, such as using an item or speaking.
Level How experienced your hero is. Your level goes up as the campaign goes on.
Magic points The fuel for spells.
Main Die The d10 you roll on every roll.
NPC A non-player character: anyone the GM plays.
Opportunity Action An attack or spell on an enemy’s turn, if the GM allows.
PC A player character: your hero.
Race / Species Your hero’s people. It gives starting traits, a bonus rank and perks.
Rank A number from 0 to 4 for each attribute. It sets your Skill Die.
Round One turn for everyone in a fight.
Session One sitting of play.
Skill Die The die you add to the Main Die. It depends on your Rank.
Trait A special skill or ability.
Wound A serious injury you can take to ignore a hit. It lowers your ranks. See Death and Survival.
Zone A rough area used instead of exact distances: Melee, Ranged or Outer.

Chapter 1: Introduction

Special Recognitions

After World is a game that stands on the shoulders of the games that came before it. These are the games that inspired much of After World’s core mechanics and themes.

  • Dungeons & Dragons 5th Edition. A game that needs no introduction, and the inspiration for most if not all tabletop games. Special thanks to the developers Gary Gygax and Wizards of the Coast.

  • A Song of Ice & Fire Roleplaying Game. A game with a great community behind it, which brings to life the popular setting created by George R. R. Martin. Special thanks to the developers Robert J. Schwalb and Green Ronin Publishing.

  • The Elder Scrolls & Fallout game series. Not tabletop games, but very famous video game series that have given many RPG fans great memories. They are the main inspiration behind After World having no classes, and behind the traits system. Special thanks to the creators, Bethesda Game Studios.

Introduction to After World

After World is a game where we all become storytellers, weaving a shared narrative together. The best way to enjoy the game is to respect each other’s choices and to try to make a good story that involves everyone.

After World encourages us to explore character differences and possible conflict. This should be done from a place of mutual respect and admiration, and only in the pursuit of character development.

Gamemasters (GMs), be aware that much of what the players can and can’t do is up to you. However, the game is intended to deliver the maximum amount of freedom to players, so keep that in mind.

The most important thing in After World is creative freedom. The game is designed to be flexible, so we can change and re-flavour things as we see fit. It is built on trust and the idea that we are all here to have fun together. Everything is a guideline, not a rule.

So begin your journey of storytelling, where your imagination is the only limit.

Chapter 2: How to Play

Each game of After World should have a flow that is similar to this:

  1. The GM describes the surroundings of the players and what options they have.

  2. The players describe what they would like to do.

  3. The GM narrates the outcomes of the players’ actions.

The game uses a D10 as the primary way to make rolls. Your ability scores determine the type of Skill dice you roll, and these are used for all actions in the game.

Main Die and Skill Dice

When you roll, you may roll two types of dice:

The Main Die is the 1d10. It is used for critical rolls and must always be rolled for every roll in the game. Skill dice add a modifier to your roll and are set by 4 Ranks:

Rank Skill die
Rank 1 1d4
Rank 2 1d6
Rank 3 1d8
Rank 4 1d12

The Skill die is rolled and added to the Main Die roll. For example, if a PC rolls to attack an enemy with a score of 4, they roll their 1d10 Main Die and check for criticals, then roll the 1d12 Skill die and add it to the total.

In-game example. Grigor the dwarf swings his axe at the Sorcerer Salazar. He rolls his attack with a Brawn of Rank 4. He first rolls his 1d10 Main Die and gets 9. He then rolls his 1d12 Skill die and gets a 7. He gets a total of 16 and beats Salazar’s Combat Defence. Grigor’s axe does 3 damage, so his damage to Salazar is well over what he needs and easily lets Grigor cut Salazar down for good… or so he thought.

Types of Rolls

  • Advantage. When given Advantage, a player may roll their dice an additional time and take the highest result.

  • Disadvantage. When given Disadvantage, a player must roll their dice again and take the lowest result.

Either effect may be given based on the encounter or situation. For example, it would be appropriate to give Disadvantage to a player climbing a tree in the rain without any equipment, and Advantage if the task were made particularly easy, such as by using climbing equipment. Advantage and Disadvantage are given as and when the GM decides.

Difficulty Score (DS). When a player rolls to get past traps or resist poisons, the roll is made against a Difficulty Score set by the GM or already set in the rules. The best way to judge how high or low the score should be is to use the same thought process as for Advantage and Disadvantage.

Actions

Players can do almost anything they can think of outside of combat, such as stocking up on supplies, investigating, speaking to other characters and much more.

During combat, players get the following actions, and all of them can be taken in the same turn. What these actions do can change at the GM’s discretion. Each character gets 1 turn every round until the combat is done.

Action Use
Main action Attack or cast a spell.
Movement Move anywhere within a zone or to another zone. Moving out of an enemy’s space gives the enemy an Opportunity Action against you, and this works both ways.
Interaction Use or equip an item, or talk in combat (max 1 minute in real life). Also lets you attack with a Light weapon.
Opportunity Action Attack or cast a spell on an enemy’s turn if the GM says so.
Surprise Action Any of the above while not seen.

Zones

Movement is tracked in After World with zones. A character may move anywhere within a zone freely on their turn. The zone types are:

  • Melee Zone. The immediate area around a player that can be reached with a melee attack.

  • Ranged Zone. The area a player cannot reach with a melee attack and can only reach with a ranged weapon. This is usually a hand length away from the player.

  • Outer Zone. The edge of the map. Players can hear nearby combat in the next zone only, and characters in this zone cannot interact with the other zones, as it is too far away, unless they have a weapon or spell with a range of 3 zones.

Characters can move to other zones based on their size, and each zone is categorised by the attack they are attempting.

Speed is the number of zones you may cross using your Movement. It is set by your size:

Size Speed
Small 1 zone
Medium 1 zone
Large 2 zones

Armour Bulk reduces Speed by 0.5 zones per point. Other effects may reduce it further. Speed can never fall below half a zone, which lets you move freely within your current zone but not leave it.

Movement Obstacles

Movement can be slowed or blocked by obstacles in a zone. Minor obstacles may cost additional movement, while serious obstacles may require a roll or another solution. The GM decides which applies.

Chapter 3: Combat

This chapter explains how attacks work in a fight. How Combat Defence is calculated, and the values for armour, weapons and shields, are in Chapter 7.

Using Combat Defence

Every creature has a Combat Defence (CD). An attack roll is made against the target’s Combat Defence. If the total of the attack roll beats it, the attack hits.

Attacks and Damage

A hit deals the damage of the weapon or power that made it, plus any bonuses. Armour Rating takes damage off each hit, and a critical hit doubles the damage (see Criticals below).

Armour and Durability

Armour can be damaged in combat. It has a durability that begins at 2 (full), falls to 1 (damaged) and then 0 (broken). It drops a tier when a hit deals damage equal to its Armour Rating +3, or through applicable traits.

Critical hits are harder on armour. Crushing critical hits set metal armour to 0 durability, and Slashing critical hits do the same for non-metal and leather armour. Both also reduce the tier-drop threshold by 2. Piercing critical hits ignore natural and powered armour and set other armour to 0. The full rules and values are in Chapter 7.

Criticals

When the Main Die is rolled and the result is a 10, it is a critical success and succeeds instantly. When the roll is a 1, it is a critical failure and fails instantly. These outcomes are absolute: whatever the total of the roll, a 10 is a success and a 1 is a failure.

  • Ability rolls. On a critical success, the goal of the roll is instantly achieved. On a critical failure, the goal is not achieved and the opposite outcome happens.

  • Combat rolls. On a critical hit, the player doubles their total damage. Example: a player uses a sword and gets a critical, and the damage is 3, so the critical damage is 3 × 2 = 6. On a critical failure, the player does no damage and the GM decides a fitting consequence.

Vulnerabilities and Resistances

If a character or creature is vulnerable to something, they take critical damage when attacked with it. If resistant, they take half damage. If immune, they take no damage.

For example, golems are vulnerable to water, so a golem that falls or is pushed into water takes critical damage until it is out of the water.

Effects

These are the effects your character may come under during a game.

Effect Description
Grappled You must make a contested Brawn roll as a full action to escape, or miss your turn.
Stunned All attacks have Advantage to hit you, your Movement is gone and you cannot use your Main action.
Grounded You must use half your Movement to get back up. While Grounded, melee attacks have Advantage to hit you, and those that hit are critical hits.
Paralysed You can’t move and you miss all turns until cured. Attacks instantly hit you and are critical hits.
Poisoned You can’t use Interactions and you fail all Health rolls not to do with the poison.
Blinded / Deafened You fail all Senses checks for that sense and can’t use Movement.
Restrained As Paralysed, but you can get free with a Brawn roll.
Frightened You must use your turn to move in the opposite direction to the creature you are frightened of.
Charmed You trust the creature that charmed you and will do anything that won’t put you in harm’s way for the duration.

Chapter 4: Fatigue and Recovery

Fatigue

A character gets points of Fatigue by over-exerting themselves, such as by casting a powerful spell or staying awake for too long. The GM decides whether a player character gets a point of Fatigue, and should use it in situations where it makes sense. The table shows how fatigued a character can become before death.

Level Effect
1 Disadvantage on ability rolls
2 Speed halved
3 Disadvantage on all rolls
4 HP max halved
5 No movement speed, Opportunity Actions, Surprise Actions or Interactions
6 Death

Rests

Players must sleep for at least 8 hours each day or suffer Fatigue points for every day they go without sleep.

Wounds cannot be healed with a rest. They must be healed by a potion or surgery, followed by a full rest.

Rest Duration Restores
Light rest 1 hour Magic points equal to your Senses or Presence score × 10, and removes any effects unless stated otherwise.
Middle rest 4 hours The same as a light rest, plus HP equal to your Health score.
Full rest 8 hours The same as a middle rest, plus heals 2 injuries.

Chapter 5: Death and Survival

Death Rolls. When a player character is reduced to 0 HP, they begin Death Rolls. They roll 1d6: above a 3 is a pass, below a 3 is a fail, and on a 3 the player rerolls. The player rolls 3 times. More passes means they get back up on 1 HP or more. More fails means their character dies. If a player character is attacked in this state, they instantly take 2 wounds. If the damage passes their maximum HP, the character dies regardless of how many Death Rolls they passed.

Wounds. Wounds affect traits. A player can take a wound to reduce damage to 0, but the penalty is that they lose 1 rank from every ability. Every wound after that takes an additional rank off every ability. A wound may also be inflicted when a player takes a critical hit. If a player is inflicted with a wound beyond the threshold, they instantly die.

Every player starts with a maximum number of wounds they can take, which is 3 for all players.

Wound Effect on all attributes
1st wound -1 Rank
2nd wound -2 Ranks
3rd wound -3 Ranks
Beyond the 3rd Death

Chapter 6: Character Creation

Making a character can look like a daunting task, but hopefully the example below will make things a little easier.

Creation Sequence

Follow these steps in order. The logic is: who the character is, then what they can do, then what they use, then what special abilities they have.

  1. Establish your character concept.
  2. Choose a race. Record its racial statistics and automatic racial traits.
  3. Choose a background. Record your training and tools.
  4. Distribute your starting attribute ranks.
  5. Calculate your derived statistics, including HP.
  6. Choose your starting weapons and armour.
  7. Review the available traits and powers in the Ability Database (Part V) and the relevant Theme.
  8. Select your starting trait or power.
  9. Complete the character sheet and check it with your GM.

You start with 10 attribute ranks. Every attribute begins at 0, you gain 1 rank each level, and no attribute can go above Rank 4. The six attributes are Brawn, Agility, Health, Smarts, Senses and Presence. Your race gives its bonus rank on top of your 10.

Starting

Players start by selecting a race and a background. Then they distribute their ranks to attributes and pick their equipment and traits.

Players should look carefully at what weapons and traits they want their character to have before distributing their attribute ranks. Once distributed, they should move on to their race and then background, and finish the character by selecting any equipment or traits from the lists provided. They should talk to their GM about any weapons, armour or traits they select to make sure the GM is happy with them.

  • Attributes. Start with 10 Ranks, and each attribute starts at 0. Every level, players get 1 Rank to spend on any of their attributes, which may only go to a max of Rank 4.

  • Race. Start with the racial stats found in the race entry.

  • Background. Start with a tool set from the background’s equipment section. The background also gives a list of things the character is trained in.

  • Weapons and armour. Start with 2 weapons you are trained in, or a power, as well as 1 set of armour.

  • Traits. Players start with their racial traits, and every level they get 1 extra trait. The GM may also award traits to characters for in-game actions.

Attributes

Attribute Used for
Brawn Any strength-based rolls (lifting a heavy object).
Agility Any speedy or agile rolls (swiping a coin purse).
Health Any rolls for staying conscious or alive in any way.
Smarts Any rolls for knowledge, remembering or complex crafting, tinkering or brewing.
Senses Any rolls for the five senses (sight, smell, hearing, touch, taste).
Presence Any rolls for talking, performing or personality.

Hit Points

Hit Points (HP) are calculated as Health dice rank × 5.

Health Dice Final HP Final HP with Healthy trait
Rank 1 5 9 + 1 every level
Rank 2 10 16 + 1 every level
Rank 3 15 23 + 1 every level
Rank 4 20 32 + 1 every level

Worked Example

Holly wants to make a level 1 character that uses a bow. She starts by picking a race, and decides on a wood elf. She writes down the racial traits and stats, then moves on to picking a background and chooses the Hunter. Next she assigns her 10 ranks to her abilities. She puts 4 into Senses, making it a 1d12, the max score and a good start for a bow user. She distributes the rest evenly and makes sure to be generous with the Health ability for her HP.

Holly then moves on to choosing her equipment and has 2 weapons and 1 armour to pick. She starts with the longbow, a perfect weapon for her archer. Next she picks a light melee weapon for up-close fighting, and lastly chooses cloth armour so she is harder to hit. Holly also starts with the Hunter tools from her background and notes that down with the rest of her equipment. She doesn’t see anything on her character sheet about traps but thinks her character would have some, so she asks her GM if she can add a bear trap. The GM listens to her reasoning and agrees to some of her equipment requests.

The last thing Holly needs to do is pick a trait. She decides on Ranger God to complete her build for an archer. She carefully looks over the core rules and ties up any other little things she may need for her character, such as carry weight, social skills and familiars.

Holly gets her character to level 2 during the session and wants to make sure she is ready for the next one. She starts by adding her 1 rank to Agility for a higher Combat Defence. Then she picks the Weapon Mastery trait with the 1 trait she gets for levelling up, to be better with her bow. She is now fully levelled up.

Chapter 7: Equipment and Inventory

The Inventory chapter defines the core rules for weapons, armour and equipment statistics. All mechanical information governing how equipment functions in play is established here. These rules are universal and apply to all After World material, regardless of theme or setting.

  • Weapons. This chapter defines how weapons function mechanically, including damage, range, handling and special traits. All weapons in After World follow this shared framework, whether ancient, modern or improvised.

  • Armour and protection. This chapter also defines how armour functions, including damage mitigation, classifications and special properties. Armour may appear in many forms, but its mechanics are always resolved using these rules.

  • Themes. Themes expand on this chapter by providing setting-specific weapons, armour and examples. They do not replace or alter the rules defined here unless explicitly stated.

In the event of a conflict, the Core System takes precedence.

Combat Defence and Armour Durability

Combat Defence (CD) is 12 + Agility Rank, less any Armour Penalty.

Agility Rank CD with no armour
Rank 1 13
Rank 2 14
Rank 3 15
Rank 4 16

  • Armour Rating. Subtracted from the damage of every hit that lands on you. A hit always deals at least half its damage, rounded up, no matter how high the target’s Armour Rating is. Armour Rating from all sources combined can never exceed 8.

  • Armour Penalty. Reduces Combat Defence by 1 to 3, depending on the armour profile.

  • Bulk. Reduces Speed by 0.5 zones per point (worn armour only).

  • Durability. Starts at 2 (full), drops to 1 (damaged) and then 0 (broken). Durability drops a tier when damage equals the Armour Rating + 3, or via traits.

  • Crushing critical hits set metal armour to 0 durability and reduce the tier-drop threshold by 2.

  • Slashing critical hits do the same for non-metal and leather armour.

  • Piercing critical hits ignore natural and powered armour, and set other armour to 0 durability.

Armour Profiles

Armour Profiles act as categories for all armour in the game. For example, the Fantasy Theme has Leather Armour, which has the Light Armour profile and therefore the same statistics shown for Light Armour. This is the same for all armour in the game, whatever the theme: all armour fits into one of these profiles.

This also means players and GMs can make any kind of armour they want and simply assign a profile that fits, giving an infinite number of possibilities for armour.

Armour Profile Tier Armour Rating Armour Penalty Bulk
None None 0 0 0
Light Armour Light 1 1 -1 0
Reinforced Armour Light 2 2 -2 0
Balanced Armour Medium 3 -2 1
Heavy Armour Heavy 1 4 -3 1
Full Armour Heavy 2 5 -3 2
Powered Armour Powered 5 -3 0/2

Weapon Tags

Weapons have tags that allow them to be used in specific ways.

Tag Effect
Light Extra attack as an Interaction (with another Light weapon).
Heavy Requires two hands.
Medium Usable one-handed or two-handed.
Hidden No conceal roll.
Reload Replenish ammo as an Interaction.
Loading Replenish ammo as a Main action.
Reach Usable at pinkie-length distance.
Throwable Throw at the specified range.
Piercing / Slashing / Crushing Accelerate armour durability degradation.
Ignore Bypasses Armour Rating for direct HP damage.
Stationary Immovable. Shift 1 zone as a full action.
DS X Target saves against the DS (fail is full effect or damage, pass is half or none).
Explosive / Burning Ignores some cover. Burning deals +1 damage per turn in the affected zone.

Weapon Profiles

Melee Profile Tags Attributes Typical Damage
Light Melee Light, Hidden, Ignore Agility (blade or whip), Brawn (axe or club) 3-4
Medium Melee Medium Agility and/or Brawn 4-6
Heavy Melee Heavy Brawn 6-7
Ranged Profile Tags Attributes Typical Damage Ranges
Light Ranged Light, Hidden, Ignore Agility 2-5 1-2 Zones
Medium Ranged Medium Agility 5-7 2 Zones
Heavy Ranged Heavy Senses (aim), Brawn (recoil) 6-7 2-3+ Zones
Siege Profile Tags Attributes Typical Damage Ranges
Siege Heavy, Loading, Stationary Agility or Senses 18-30+ Half-4 Zones

Shield and Thrown Profiles

Shield Profile Tags Combat Defence modifier Damage
Light Shield Light +1 1
Medium Shield Medium +2 2
Heavy Shield Heavy +3 3
Thrown Profile DS Attributes Typical Damage Ranges
Blast Thrown 12-13 Agility 6-12 damage (half on pass) Half-1 Zone
Debuff Thrown 12-13 Agility or Health Status effect (Stunned, Blinded, etc.) on fail Half Zone
Zone Thrown 12-13 Health or Senses Ongoing effect while in zone (for example 2 damage per turn, Blinded) 1 Zone (persistent)

Chapter 8: Traits

Traits act as skills or abilities that characters learn or gain during the course of the game. Some traits are available from the start of the game, such as a racial trait or the traits you pick at level 1. These are what your character has learned before the game.

Your character will gain traits as you level up, or may gain a trait in the game depending on the type of game you are playing. Most games will have your character gain 1 trait every level.

If you feel there is not a trait that fits what you want for your character, consider reworking an existing one, or talk to your GM and see if a new one can be made.

The complete catalogue of traits and abilities is in Part V, the Ability Database. Each Theme also says which traits it uses.

Chapter 9: Additional Rules

Carry Weight

A player’s carry weight is Brawn rank × 5. Equipment weights are Light = 1, Medium = 3 and Heavy = 6.

Brawn Rank Carry weight
1 5
2 10
3 15
4 20

Crafting

When you craft an item, you must make a roll relevant to how you are crafting it against a DS from the table below. The type of material used determines what skill level is needed. GMs are encouraged to use this table as a guide to implement their own crafting rules and systems.

This system can be used with any Theme, as most settings allow for some form of item creation.

Skill Level Difficulty Score
Novice 7
Adept 8
Master 10

Bribery

You may bribe NPCs or fellow players with money during the game. The table shows how much money is required, based on the target’s wealth, to avoid a -5 to the roll.

Target’s Lifestyle Money required
Absolute Poverty 1
Poverty 10
Poor 50
Modest 100
Comfortable 500
Wealthy 1000
Aristocrat 10,000

Ships

Whether they are starships in space or wooden vessels on the seas, ship statistics can be found here and may be used as written, or as a template or guide for custom rules.

Ship HP CD Armour Weapon Attack Damage Zones
1 man 8 16 0 Turret 1d12 2 Small
10 man 15 13 7/7 Turret 1d12 8 Medium
30 man 25 11 14/14 Cannon 1d12 16 Large
50 man 50 8 25/25 Cannon 1d12 30 2 per move

Chapter 10: Quick Reference and Sample Characters

Quick Reference

The most common values and rules in one place. Each entry points to the full rules.

A roll

Step What to do
1 Say what you try. The GM names the attribute and the target.
2 Roll the d10. A 10 is a success. A 1 is a failure.
3 Otherwise roll the Skill Die for your Rank and add it.
4 Add bonuses (trained tool +3, traits and so on).
5 Beat the target: Combat Defence for attacks, DS for everything else.

Skill Dice

Rank 0 1 2 3 4
Die None d4 d6 d8 d12

Your turn

Main action Attack or cast a spell
Movement Move within a zone or to another zone
Interaction Use or equip an item, speak, or make a Light-weapon extra attack
Opportunity Action Attack on an enemy’s turn, if the GM allows it
Surprise Action Any action while unseen

Damage

Step What to do
1 Attack total beats Combat Defence
2 Damage = weapon + bonuses (double on a critical hit)
3 Subtract Armour Rating, but never below half (rounded up)
4 Subtract from HP

Dying

0 HP Roll a d6 three times: above 3 passes, below 3 fails, a 3 rerolls. More passes: back up on 1 HP. More fails: death.
Wounds Take one to turn a hit into 0 damage. −1, −2, −3 ranks to all attributes. A fourth Wound is death.

Key numbers

HP Health Rank × 5
Combat Defence 12 + Agility Rank − armour penalty
Carry weight Brawn Rank × 5 (Light 1, Medium 3, Heavy 6)
Magic DS 10 + Senses or Presence Rank
Sleep 8 hours a day, or gain Fatigue

Rests

Rest Time What it does
Light rest 1 hour Restores magic points equal to your Senses or Presence Rank × 10, and removes most effects.
Middle rest 4 hours A light rest, plus HP equal to your Health score.
Full rest 8 hours A middle rest, plus healing of 2 injuries.

Full rules: Rests and Death and Survival.

Attributes

Attribute Use it for
Brawn Strength rolls
Agility Speed and agile rolls, and Combat Defence
Health Staying conscious and alive, and HP
Smarts Knowledge, remembering, crafting, tinkering and brewing
Senses The five senses
Presence Talking, performing and personality

Sample Characters

Four complete level 1 characters for the Fantasy Theme that you can use as examples or starting points. Each follows the Creation Sequence in Chapter 6. Ranks shown include the bonus rank from the character’s race, which comes on top of the 10.

Brogan Ironhide: the knight

Kolskon knight. Tough, strong and good at the front of a fight.

Carry weight. Each hero’s gear fits within their carry weight (Brawn Rank × 5). If you swap in heavier gear, add up the weights as you go.

Chapter 11: Understanding Themes

The Core System holds the rules every game uses. A Theme adds the setting. This chapter explains how the Themes in this book work and how to put them together into a campaign.

What Is a Theme?

A Theme is a setting for After World. It sits on top of the Core System and adds the races or species, backgrounds, themed traits, equipment and extra rules that give the setting its character. This book has three Themes: Fantasy (Part II), Modern (Part III) and Star Wars (Part IV). Every Theme uses the same Core System, so the same dice, combat, characters and equipment rules apply in all of them.

How Themes Work

The guiding idea is simple: the Theme establishes the setting, and optional rules establish its character.

The GM chooses a Theme and then its setting selector:

  • Fantasy: a Fantasy Type.
  • Modern: a year or period.
  • Star Wars: an Era.

The GM then chooses any Feature Modules they want, and tells the players what is active before they create their characters. The standard content of a Theme, such as its races, backgrounds, traits, inventory and loot, is available according to the GM’s approval.

Theme Modules

A Feature Module is an optional rule set that adds one system to a Theme, such as Magic, Creatures of the Night, Superpowers or the Force. Each Feature Module is self-contained and has its own introduction, contents and rules.

The GM may choose any combination of modules, including none. Modules are guidelines, not mandatory packages. Each module suggests which Types, periods or Eras it fits best, but those suggestions never restrict a table. GMs are encouraged to create, adapt and change modules.

Magic, the Force and Superpowers sit in the same place as the other Feature Modules in their Themes, but each keeps its own mechanics and terms.

Rules Precedence

When two rules disagree, the more specific book wins. The order, weakest to strongest, is:

Core System → Theme → Feature Module → GM house rule

The Star Wars Theme adds more steps inside it: SW-BASE, then an Era module, then a Feature module. The one exception is the equipment framework in Chapter 7. Themes expand it with setting-specific weapons, armour and examples, and they do not replace or alter its rules unless they say so explicitly. If there is a conflict over equipment rules, the Core System takes precedence.

Optional Rules and Expansions

Everything in After World is a guideline, not a rule. Feature Modules, Types, periods, Eras and optional rules such as Tiered Force are only in play when the GM switches them on. A GM may drop a rule, change it or write a new one, so long as the table agrees.

Combining Themes and Modules

Modules can be combined freely. The exception is Star Wars Eras: only one Era is active at a time because each one redefines which species, gear and factions are common. A GM may switch Eras between campaign acts.

Every weapon and armour should still work in mixed-Theme play, at the GM’s discretion. A GM may also allow a race or trait from one Theme in another, but should think about it more carefully, because races and traits are tuned for their own setting. Fantasy equipment may be used in other Themes at the GM’s discretion, and the Star Wars Conversion Guide gives equivalents between Fantasy and Star Wars.

Building a Campaign

  1. Choose the Theme.
  2. Choose its setting selector: a Fantasy Type, a Modern period or a Star Wars Era.
  3. Choose any Feature Modules you want.
  4. Tell the players what is active before they make characters.
  5. Set the power level through the world, the enemies and the loot. The setting selector does not decide how powerful the characters are.

Part II — Fantasy Theme

The fantasy setting for the After World Core System.

Introduction

The Fantasy Theme adds a fantasy setting to the Core System (Part I): races, backgrounds, themed traits, equipment, alchemy and loot, plus four optional Feature Modules. Everything in Part I still applies unless this Part says otherwise.

To start a Fantasy campaign, the GM chooses a Fantasy Type to set the genre and atmosphere, then chooses any Feature Modules they want. Players then make characters using the races, backgrounds and equipment in this Part, and the traits and powers in the Ability Database (Part V).

Rules Precedence

Core System → Fantasy Theme → Feature Module → GM house rule. When two rules disagree, the more specific book wins. See Rules Precedence in Chapter 11.

What Is in This Part

Section What it holds
Fantasy Types The five Types a GM chooses from to set genre and atmosphere.
Races, Backgrounds, Traits, Inventory, Alchemy, Loot The standard Fantasy content every game can use.
Magic Optional Feature Module: alignment, enchanting, spell crafting and the spell lists.
Summoning Optional Feature Module: familiars and undead.
Arts and Forms Optional Feature Module: Sword Forms, Martial Arts and Performer Forms.
Creatures of the Night Optional Feature Module: vampires, werewolves and the Returned.

Theme-specific content

The races and traits in this Theme are made for a fantasy setting and may only be chosen in a campaign that uses this Theme, because they may not be balanced for other Themes.

Every weapon and armour should still work in mixed-Theme play, at the GM’s discretion. A GM may also allow a race or trait from this Theme in another Theme, but should think about it more carefully.

Example Campaign

A GM wants a spooky, tragic story set in a country of old castles and dark forests. They choose the Gothic Fantasy Type. They switch on two Feature Modules: Creatures of the Night, because the villains are vampires, and Magic, because one player wants to play a healer. They tell the players what is active before character creation. The players choose races and backgrounds from this Part, and the GM sets the power level through the enemies they face and the loot they find.

Fantasy Types

The GM chooses a Fantasy Type to establish the genre and atmosphere of the campaign. Types are starting frameworks, not strict rule sets. They do not restrict races, backgrounds, equipment or Feature Modules, and the GM may mix or change them. The five Types are:

  • Sword and Sorcery
  • High Fantasy
  • Dark Fantasy
  • Gothic Fantasy
  • Mythic Fantasy

Races

Choose a lineage that shapes your character’s abilities, culture and place in the world. Each race has a short description and the same kind of stats: a starting trait, ability ranks, a perk, languages and a size. The chapter also covers the elven sub-races, human peoples, beast races, fey, constructed beings and holy and unholy lineages.

Races are tuned to the same power level as the Star Wars species. A race gives you one starting trait, Rank 1 in one ability, one perk, languages and a size. A race that is short on one of these gets a bigger perk, and a strong perk comes with a limit such as once per day. Where a line says or 1 extra trait, you may take any trait instead.

Damar

Stats

  • Start with Senator trait or 1 extra trait

  • Rank 1 in Presence ability

  • Bird familiar: start with a Bird familiar (see Familiars, no Magic Point cost)

  • Languages: Basic, 1 extra

  • Size: Medium

The Damars are wise, discerning people of moderate height and build, with tawny to fair skin and mostly brown or black hair. They are respected for strategic thinking and political skill, and they excel at negotiating peaceful ends to conflict by weighing many viewpoints and long-term consequences.

Celisen

Stats

  • Start with Disarm trait

  • Rank 1 in Agility ability

  • +1 to hit and damage with all Light weapons or 1 extra trait

  • Languages: Basic, 1 extra

  • Size: Medium

Celisen are desert nomads, slightly shorter and more slender than other humans, with dusky brown skin and fine silks. They are skilled hunters, trackers and warriors, quick-witted and resourceful, and they know the poisons of the desert better than anyone.

Marroar

Stats

  • Start with Heart Of Oak trait

  • Rank 1 in Senses ability

  • +1 to hit and damage with 1H swords and pistols or 1 extra trait

  • Languages: Basic, 1 extra

  • Size: Medium

The Marroar are a seafaring people, tall and slender with pale skin, fair hair and green or brown eyes. They value self-sufficiency, read currents and tides with ease, and have formed alliances with so many other races that they are the most widespread of the human peoples.

Taro

Stats

  • Start with Druid trait

  • Rank 1 in Presence ability

  • Hound familiar: start with a Hound familiar (see Familiars, no Magic Point cost)

  • Languages: Basic, 1 extra

  • Size: Medium

The Taro are a close-knit people, fiercely loyal to friends and family. They are tall and muscular, with dark mahogany skin, curly black hair and clothes of natural materials. They believe all living things are connected, and they use that knowledge to make powerful medicines and to tame wild animals.

Kolskon

Stats

  • Start with Healthy trait

  • Rank 1 in Health ability

  • +1 to hit and damage with all melee weapons or 1 extra trait

  • Languages: Basic, 1 extra

  • Size: Medium

Kolskon are warriors and poets, children of the storm, with pale to rosy skin, blue or steel-grey eyes and flowing blond hair. They value honour above all else, and their clans are led by chieftains chosen for combat prowess.

High Elf

Stats

  • Start with Foresight trait or 1 extra trait

  • Rank 1 in Senses ability

  • Arcane blood: 10 extra magic points, or Advantage on rolls to resist magic effects (choose one)

  • Languages: Basic, Elvish

  • Size: Medium

High elves are the children of the light, tall and slender, with sun-kissed skin and eyes like golden pools. They are scholars and mages, proud and ambitious, who keep to their own spired cities and strive for perfection in all they do.

Wood Elf

Stats

  • Start with Bullseye trait

  • Rank 1 in Agility ability

  • Woodsman: Advantage on Stealth rolls in forests

  • Languages: Basic, Elvish

  • Size: Medium

Wood elves are the children of the forest, with leaf-green skin and sharp eyes. They are masters of the bow, skilled trackers and woodsmen, and they use every part of the animals they kill, often making bone masks to mark the passage into adulthood.

Snow Elf

Stats

  • Start with Foresight trait or 1 extra trait

  • Rank 1 in Health ability

  • Cold-born: resistance to cold damage and effects, and Advantage with slingshots

  • Languages: Basic, Elvish

  • Size: Medium

Snow elves are an offshoot of the High Elves who adapted to the arctic. They have pearl-white skin, white or silver eyes and tall, stocky builds, and they are known for physical and mental strength, as statesmen and as hunters. They live in towers of stone and wood and have good relations with the Kolskon.

Red Elf

Stats

  • Start with Angel Outlaw trait or 1 extra trait

  • Rank 1 in Presence ability

  • Can see through any type of smoke or fog

  • Languages: Basic, Elvish

  • Size: Medium

Red elves have dark red or orange skin, blood-coloured eyes and black hair. Theirs is a proud matriarchal society ruled by tyrannical ash priests who live in volcanoes, and they are hated for their tradition of slavery. Some have rejected slavery and live in peace with other races.

Moon Elf

Stats

  • Rank 1 in Senses ability

  • Stealth spell: gain the Stealth spell once a day with no Magic Point cost. Creatures looking for you make a Senses roll against DS 12 + your Agility rank

  • Moonbound: Advantage on Senses rolls at night, Disadvantage on Senses rolls in the day

  • Languages: Basic, Elvish

  • Size: Medium

Moon elves are the most secretive of the elves. They live in hidden cities that burrow underground at dawn and rise as soaring towers when the moon is out. They have pale blue skin with glowing white markings that shift with the moon, and a religion shrouded in mystery that is said to give them an affinity for stealth magic.

Dark Elf

Stats

  • Start with Angel Outlaw trait or 1 extra trait

  • Rank 1 in Agility ability

  • Can see through any type of darkness

  • Languages: Basic, Elvish

  • Size: Medium

Dark elves are a shrouded people from the deep lands. They are tall and slender, with ashen grey to obsidian skin, raven black or snow white hair, red glowing eyes and sharp teeth. They rarely come to the surface, and when they do they are feared and mistrusted for old raids on surface settlements.

Dwarf

Stats

  • Start with Crafter trait or 1 extra trait

  • Rank 1 in Brawn ability

  • Honourable strike: once per day, after you defeat an enemy honourably, your next hit is a critical

  • +1 to hit with all Light weapons

  • Languages: Basic, Dwarvish

  • Size: Small

Dwarves are the short, stocky miners and craftsmen of the world, with great strength and a love of gold and jewels. Their greatest treasure is friends and family. They are stubborn and short-tempered, but fair, just and always true to their word.

Halfling

Stats

  • Start with Standing In Shadow trait

  • Rank 1 in Agility ability

  • Light-footed: once per combat, move without provoking Opportunity Actions

  • Advantage on cooking and farming rolls or 1 extra trait

  • Languages: Basic, Halfling

  • Size: Small

Halflings are cheerful, industrious farmers who love food, hospitality and a shared meal. They are short and slender with curly hair and leathery soles that make them natural sneaks, and they live in close-knit villages and hamlets.

Eltieam

Stats

  • Rank 1 in Presence ability

  • Silver Tongue: gain the Mind Trick power once a day. The DS is 12 + your Presence rank

  • Flanker: once per combat, move an ally to a flanking position as an Opportunity Action

  • Languages: Basic, Eltie

  • Size: Medium

The Eltieam are a race of fire and steel, with crimson skin, shrouded black hair and metallic eyes. They are leaders and warriors, masters of tactical warfare and social interaction, whose empire is one of the greatest in the world. They are also ruthless and ambitious, and will stop at nothing to expand it.

Orc

Stats

  • Rank 1 in Brawn ability

  • Thick hide: resistant to Piercing

  • Flesh-eater: once per combat, spend an Interaction to eat flesh from a dead foe and gain HP equal to the foe’s Health rank

  • Languages: Basic, Orcish

  • Size: Medium or Large (choose when you make your character)

Orcs are fearsome warriors known for strength, ferocity and love of battle. They are tall and muscular, with green skin, large tusks and hair from long and flowing to short and bristly. Often seen as outcasts for raiding and pillaging, they are a complex people who are resourceful, adaptable and fiercely loyal to family.

Half-Giant

Stats

  • Start with Healthy trait

  • Rank 1 in Brawn ability

  • Stubborn flesh: once per day, ignore 1 failed Death Roll

  • Languages: Basic, Giant

  • Size: Large

Half-giants are the children of giants and mortals, taller and stronger than most races, with legendary tolerance for pain. They have pale skin, cold-coloured eyes, no hair, and horns across their bodies, and they may wear bone or metal ornaments. They are often feared, but those who know them speak of their loyalty and courage.

Ophidian

Stats

  • Rank 1 in Presence or Agility ability

  • Shapeshift: gain the Shapeshift power once a day. The roll is Smarts against DS 12 + your Presence rank

  • Serpent blood: resistant to poison

  • Languages: Basic, Serpent tongue

  • Size: Medium

Ophidians are elusive snake-like humanoids, known for cunning, agility and shapeshifting. Many infiltrate every level of society for ill purposes, though some use their powers to protect those who cannot protect themselves. All are dangerous, and most cannot be trusted.

Race Variants

These variants can be chosen instead of the standard entry.

Variant Stats
Half-Breed 1 trait from main race. 1 rank and 1 trait from secondary race. Languages: Basic and secondary race. Size: main race
Eltieam (martial) Rank 1 in Brawn or Agility. Weapon Mastery trait. Flanker perk as the Eltieam. Replaces Silver Tongue. Languages: Basic, Eltie. Size: Medium
Ancient Blooded Replace your race’s first trait with: you can see magical alignment and track it as a free action. All other stats come from the race
Moon Elf (moonlit) As Moon Elf, but replace the Stealth spell with: when in moonlight you can instantly become hidden and can only be seen with magic
Orc (Rampage) Rampage trait. +2 to hit with Clubs and Maces. Once per combat, when you are knocked down you can get back up on 1 HP. Languages: Basic, Orcish. Size: Medium or Large
Marroar (Celtic/Saxon) Rampage trait. Rank 1 in Senses. +1 to hit and damage with 1H axes and longbows or 1 extra trait. Languages: Basic, 1 extra. Size: Medium

Beast Races

Anthropomorphic humanoid creatures that are intelligent and sentient but rarer than the races above. Beast races have natural weapons (see Natural weapons below).

Luani (Lion humanoid)

  • Rank 1 in Brawn ability

  • Claws and bite (see Natural weapons)

  • Pounce: once per combat, lunge forward and deal double damage on a hit, but the roll to hit has a -4

  • Languages: Basic, Beast Speak

  • Size: Medium

Felani (Cat humanoid)

  • Rank 1 in Agility ability

  • Claws and bite (see Natural weapons)

  • Nimble: 1 zone climb speed, resistant to fall damage, and Advantage on rolls to save yourself from falling

  • Languages: Basic, Beast Speak

  • Size: Medium

Dindie (Wolf humanoid)

  • Start with Target trait

  • Rank 1 in Senses ability

  • Claws and bite (see Natural weapons)

  • Languages: Basic, Beast Speak

  • Size: Medium

Exotic Races

Rare humanoid creatures that are intelligent and sentient but only exist in forgotten places or specific climates.

Fairy (Fey)

  • Rank 1 in Presence ability

  • Small form: once a day, take a small form that does not provoke Opportunity Actions, squeezes into small spaces, gives +3 to Combat Defence and a fly speed of 1 zone. While in small form, being hit makes you instantly unconscious

  • Nature’s gift: gain the Heal power with +2 to your healing pool

  • Languages: Fey

  • Size: Small

Spriggan (Fey)

As the Fairy, except the Heal power is swapped for the Shapeshift power at half cost, and the size is Medium.

Saurian (Reptile or Dragon humanoid)

  • Rank 1 in Health ability

  • Breath weapon (see Racial abilities)

  • Claws and bite (see Natural weapons)

  • Languages: Basic, Drake speech or Serpent tongue

  • Size: Medium

Constructed Races

Constructed races follow the same approach as droids in the Star Wars book: strong immunities, paid for by a weakness.

Golem

  • Rank 1 in Brawn, Health or Smarts

  • Does not need to eat, drink, sleep or breathe

  • Immune to poison, diseases and curses

  • Vulnerable to water

  • Takes Wounds as normal

  • Languages: Basic, Divine

  • Size: Medium

Zombie / Undead

  • Rank 1 in Brawn, Health or Presence

  • Does not need to eat, drink, sleep or breathe

  • Immune to poison, diseases and demonic magic

  • Vulnerable to fire

  • Takes Wounds as normal

  • Languages: Basic, Divine

  • Size: Medium

Holy and Unholy Races

Races that exist in, or have blood from, the Angelic or Demonic realms.

Half-Angel

  • Rank 1 in Presence ability

  • When knocked down in combat, get back up on 1 HP once every middle rest

  • Immune to Angelic magic

  • Languages: Basic, Divine

  • Size: Medium

Half-Demon

  • Rank 1 in Presence ability

  • Shapeshift: gain the Shapeshift power once a day. The roll is Smarts against DS 12 + your Presence rank

  • Resistant to fire and immune to extreme heat conditions

  • Languages: Basic, Devil Speech

  • Size: Medium

Custom Race

Rank 1 in 1 ability and 2 traits. Languages: Basic and 1 extra. Size: agree with your GM.

Natural weapons and racial abilities

Natural weapons are Light Melee weapons. Claws are Slashing and a bite is Piercing. Claws and bite count as one weapon for the Light tag, so you cannot make two natural attacks in one turn unless you also hold another Light weapon.

Natural weapon Saurian Dindie Luani Felani
Bite 4 damage, Brawn 4 damage, Agility or Brawn 3 damage, Brawn 3 damage, Agility
Claw 3 damage, Agility or Brawn 3 damage, Agility or Brawn 4 damage, Brawn 3 damage, Agility
Racial ability Effect
Saurian breath weapon Once a day. DS 10 + level, range 2 zones in a straight line, 12 damage or half on a pass. Damage goes up by 3 at 5th and 7th level

Backgrounds

Backgrounds are your character’s profession or knowledge before the game starts. Pick 1 background, or make your own by choosing 1 tool set and training from the existing ones, or talk to the GM about creating an entirely new one.

Not being trained in an item or task gives Disadvantage on all rolls involving it. Each background tool gives +3 to rolls involving the tasks it is used for. For example, a Scoundrel trained in a lockpicking kit adds +3 to rolls for lockpicking, but may not be trained in horse riding and has Disadvantage on those rolls.

These bonuses are for certain rolls and do not include attacks or damage. The Disadvantage does apply to weapons a character is not trained in.

Knight

You are dedicated to an organisation or army and have committed to uphold the tenets of your chosen allegiance. You keep a vow, for example sworn to valour, protect the weak, or speak only truth. These are only examples, and the vow must not be broken or you will be disgraced. People know you as a hero or villain, and your name carries weight.

  • Trained in: Knight tools, all weapons, all armour

  • Knight tools: anything used for battle planning, scouting enemies, horse riding and other military acts. Includes everything in the Scoundrel equipment

  • Equipment: Heavy, 5 torches, soldier rank, repair kit, extra carry pouches (+10), banner, riding horse

Scoundrel

You know the underbelly of society and many of its less reputable members. Playing by the rulebook is against everything you know to be true, and the only rules that matter are the ones that put you ahead. You are no stranger to violence, theft and other crime, though you may or may not be a criminal yourself.

  • Trained in: Scoundrel tools, light weapons, armour with Bulk 0 to 2

  • Scoundrel tools: anything used for lockpicking, forgery, disguises and other criminal and roguish acts

  • Equipment: Heavy, extra carry pouches (+10), 5 torches, lockpicking kit, forgery kit, climbing gear, caltrops, disguise kit, 100 ft of rope, 10 ft of string, 1 smoke bomb, grappling hook

Sage

You have studied or meditated on life’s greatest mysteries and scour the corners of the world for knowledge only a select few possess. You learn quickly, read between the lines and solve the hardest puzzles. Your obsession may be body, mind or magic.

  • Trained in: Sage tools, light weapons, armour with Bulk 0 to 3, staff

  • Sage tools: anything used for research, meditation, prayer and other mental or spiritual acts

  • Equipment: Medium, roll of parchment, filled inkwell, quill, religious code or book of study, 10 sticks of incense, flint and steel, 10 ft of string, world map

Herbalist

You know how to harness the natural world to make soothing potions and potent poisons. Plants, flowers and fungi are your surroundings, and you know them better than people. You can bring people back from the brink with a few crushed leaves, and many pay for your concoctions and knowledge.

  • Trained in: Alchemist tools, light weapons, armour with Bulk 0 to 2, potions

  • Alchemist tools: anything used for brewing medicines, poisons, herbs and other alchemical acts

  • Equipment: Heavy, 5 healing potions, medical equipment, roll of parchment, filled inkwell, quill, 4 prosthetic limbs, brewing equipment, mortar and pestle, sack of 10 healing herbs, 10 sticks of incense

Hunter

You know the wild and can track the creatures that roam it. You spend more time around your prey than people, know traps and weaponry, and are no stranger to sleeping rough for weeks to follow a pack or a lone predator. You are a trained killer even when you choose not to kill.

  • Trained in: Hunter tools, all weapons, all armour, bombs, traps

  • Hunter tools: anything used for traps, tracking, foraging and other animal and nature acts

  • Equipment: Medium, grappling hook, 1 smoke bomb, 10 torches, a large net, 5 sets of traps, climbing gear, 100 ft of rope, caltrops, 20 bait, world map, unique item from prey x3

Craftsman

You have a trade and you are good at it. You can make weapons, armour, traps, structures and everyday items, and many will know your work from your maker’s mark. Working with weapons and armour means you are used to them and can tell when they need work.

  • Trained in: Craftsman tools, all weapons, all armour, traps, bombs, gauntlets, staff, double weapons

  • Craftsman tools: anything used for repairing, forging, constructing and other crafting and tinkering acts

  • Equipment: Heavy, repair kit, blacksmithing kit, item materials (20), 5 torches, hammer, anvil, portable forge, bellows, sack of 100 coals, stone cutter’s kit, tinkering kit

Noble

You come from a wealthy and influential family and know the political world better than the battlefield. You have people with stock in money-making ventures and sway in those circles, and a clear advantage in social and formal settings. You have never known a hard life, and your purse buys a room in the finest inns.

  • Trained in: Noble tools, light weapons, cloth and non-metal armour

  • Noble tools: anything used for city management, writing, linguistics and other political and social acts

  • Equipment: Heavy, roll of parchment, filled inkwell, quill, forgery kit, lockpicking kit, disguise kit, official noble seal, city map, world map, unique item

Explorer

You have travelled the known world, sailing the oceans and climbing the tallest peaks. You know cultures far beyond where you were born, can read the weather before it changes, and treat maps like a second language. You know how to survive in the wild and where to make camp.

  • Trained in: Explorer tools, light weapons, armour with Bulk 0 to 2, gauntlets, whips

  • Explorer tools: anything used for navigation, cartography, weather reading and other survival and travelling acts

  • Equipment: Heavy, lockpicking kit, 10 torches, grappling hook, preservation kit, extra carry pouches (+10), compass, sundial, climbing gear, 200 ft of rope, world map, winter gear

Traits

Traits act as skills or abilities that characters learn or gain during the game. Some are available from the start, such as a racial trait or the traits you pick at level 1. These are what your character has learned before the game.

Your character gains traits as you level up, or may gain a trait in the game depending on the type of game you are playing. Most games give 1 trait every level.

If no trait fits what you want for your character, consider reworking an existing one, or talk to your GM and see if a new one can be made.

The Fantasy traits are in the Ability Database, in the Fantasy Traits section, with the core and combat traits.

Inventory

Your inventory is the weapons, armour, tools, relics and belongings your character carries through the fractured world of After World. All mechanics for weapons, armour, carry weight and equipment weights stay in the Core System. This chapter only gives fantasy examples that use those profiles without changing them.

In the fantasy age, possessions are earned, inherited or taken by force. A warrior may carry a chipped longsword passed down through generations, while a wanderer’s pack might hold dried meat, ritual charms and a half-legible map. Coin is heavy, armour is cumbersome, and magic, when it exists, is rarely convenient.

Inventory also reflects culture. A knight’s kit differs from that of a desert nomad, hedge mage or raider. When managing equipment, always follow the Core System. In a conflict, the Core System takes precedence.

Armour and shields

For full statistics see the Core System’s Armour Profiles.

Full plate Full Armour (Heavy 2) Noble’s or king’s battle armour
Enchanted armour or robes Powered Armour Bulk ignored when charged with Arc crystal
Shield Profile Flavour notes

Weapons

Weapons in the fantasy age are shaped by tradition, craftsmanship and war. For attack resolution, damage, ranges and weapon tags, always refer to the Core System. These lists use those rules without altering them.

Alchemy

Alchemy works the same as the Core System crafting system. A potion or poison can heal or damage for half the regular amount, or have an effect that is not otherwise specified, such as paralysis or blindness, for a set duration.

Level DS Damage or effect Range Duration
Novice 8 8 1 zone 3 turns
Adept 9 10 2 zones 4 turns
Master 11 15 3 zones 5 turns

Potions and poisons

Potions are listed by the level at which a party is likely to find or make them.

Potions and poisons, Levels 1 to 2

Item Category Effect
Minor Healing Draught Potion Heals HP equal to the user’s Health Rank +1. Essential for treating injuries.
Antidote Vial Potion Instantly removes the Poisoned effect.
Glow-Liquid Potion Provides light in a Melee Zone for 1 hour when applied to an object.
Paralytic Spores Poison Target must roll Health vs DS 7 or become Grounded (hits are criticals).
Stink-Bomb Poison Creates a 1-zone cloud; those inside gain Disadvantage on Senses rolls.
Smell-Masking Herb Potion Grants Advantage on Stealth rolls against tracking for 1 hour.
Soothing Salve Potion Removes 1 point of Fatigue (applicable for Level 1 fatigue only).
Standard Adrenaline Shot Potion Instantly restores Movement Action; gives 1 Fatigue point after 10 minutes.
Basic Solvent Compound Dissolves non-metallic Novice-tier adhesives or locks.
Weakness Toxin Poison Target deals -1 damage on all successful hits for one encounter.
Blessed Water Potion Deals 4 Damage to ‘Creatures of the Night’ (Undead/Werewolves).
Iron-Gut Brew Potion Advantage on Health rolls against ingested poisons for 4 hours.
Quick-Clot Powder Potion Prevents a player at 0 HP from taking Wounds for 1 round.
Numbing Paste Potion Negates Disadvantage from Level 1 Fatigue for 10 minutes.

Potions and poisons, Levels 3 to 4

Item Category Effect

Elixir of Agility Potion Increases Agility Rank by 1 (max 4) for 1 hour.

Weakening Acid Poison Reduces target’s AR by 2 permanently (only non-magical armour).
Smoke Pellet Compound Creates a 1-zone cloud; provides total cover for Surprise Actions.
Senses Booster Potion Increases Senses Rank by 1; allows hearing in Outer Zones.
Berserker’s Brew Potion +2 Damage but attacks against the user have Advantage.
Mage-Bane Dust Poison Target must roll Senses vs DS 8 or lose 20 Magic Points.
Frost-Coating Potion Weapon deals Cold damage; critical hits inflict Stunned effect.
Liquid Courage Potion Immune to the Frightened effect for 1 hour.
Bark-Skin Resin Potion AR: +2. If hit by fire, user takes Critical Damage.
Tracker’s Scent Poison Marked target can be seen through smoke/fog by the user.
Taro Medicine Potion Removes Stunned or Charmed status effect.
Corrosive Vapour Poison Deals 2 damage to all in a Melee Zone at the start of their turn.
Focusing Salts Potion Negates Disadvantage on ability rolls for 1 minute.

Potions and poisons, Levels 5 to 6

Item Category Effect
Regeneration Serum Potion Heals 2 HP at the start of every turn for 5 turns.
Invisibility Tonic Potion Grants automatic stealth success for 1 minute.
Celisen Desert Venom Poison Inflicts Poisoned: No interactions and fail all Health & Brawn rolls.

Explosive Flask Compou nd Deals 6 Damage to all targets in a Melee Zone.
Dragon’s Breath Potion User may use a Main Action to deal 5 Fire Damage in a 1-zone cone.
Greater Antidote Potion Cures Poisoned, Paralysed, and Restrained.
Brawn Mutagen Potion Increases Brawn Rank by 1; user becomes ‘Large’ size for 10 min.
Ghost-Step Oil Potion Allows movement through enemies without triggering Opportunity Actions.
Petrifying Oil Poison Target must roll Health vs DS 10 or become Paralysed for 1 minute.
Willpower Extract Potion Instantly restores all Magic Points.
Heavy Metal Toxin Poison Target gains 2 Fatigue points instantly.
Vulnerability Mist Poison Target becomes Vulnerable to a chosen damage type for 1 combat.
Fortify Health Potion Increases HP maximum by the user’s Health Rank for 1 hour.

Almora’s Brew Potion If reduced to 0 HP, user instantly stabilises on max HP.

Loot

Loot tables are ranked by party level, 1 to 7. Loot should fit the place and the enemy, and the GM does not have to roll for every item.

Profiles. Loot weapons and armour use the Core System profiles and the lists in the Inventory chapter. At level 5 and above, a weapon or armour may be Masterwork: a weapon gets +1 damage and armour gets +1 Armour Rating. Armour Rating from all sources can never exceed 8.
