Droids may take any trait that makes sense for a machine. Traits about biology, such as natural healing, do not work unless the GM agrees there is a mechanical equivalent. These five are available when FT-DROIDS is on, in the same layout as the entries in the Ability Database (Part V).

The five droid traits are catalogued in the Ability Database (Part V). Each links to its full entry.

Code Trait Entry
SW-008 Integrated Tools Integrated Tools
SW-009 Reinforced Chassis Reinforced Chassis
SW-010 Droid Interface Droid Interface
SW-011 Self-Repair Self-Repair
SW-012 Memory Bank Memory Bank

Maintenance and Upgrades

Droid damage and repair

Repairs use the Core System crafting rules. The repairer rolls Smarts, with +3 for Technician tools, against the DS for the work.

Repair Needs DS Effect
Field repair Repair kit or Technician tools 7 Full Action. Restore HP equal to the repairer’s Smarts Rank x 2.
Structural repair Repair kit, 5 item materials, 1 hour 8 Restore the droid to full HP.
Wound repair Workshop, replacement parts, 8 hours 8 Remove 1 Wound.
Memory recovery Compatible computer, 1 hour 10 Recover the memory core of a destroyed droid.
Full reconstruction Workshop, parts worth 1,000 credits, 24 hours 10 Rebuild a destroyed droid with its memory core.

On a failed Memory recovery or Full reconstruction roll, the droid returns with part of its memory or personality changed. The GM decides what is lost.

Upgrades

Upgrades use the crafting rules. A droid or companion may carry up to 3 upgrades. A Master-level upgrade counts as 2. No upgrade can raise an ability above Rank 4.

Upgrade Level DS Effect
Hidden compartment Novice 7 Hides 1 Light item. Searching for it needs Senses DS 12 + the droid’s Smarts Rank.
Tool attachment Novice 7 Integrates one tool set. +3 to its tasks.
Translator module Novice 7 Adds 2 languages.
Improved sensors Adept 8 +1 Senses Rank.
Servo overhaul Adept 8 +1 Brawn or Agility Rank, your choice when fitted.
Reinforced plating Adept 8 +1 Armour Rating and -1 CD.
Weapon mount Adept 8 Mounts one Light or Medium Ranged weapon. Fired as a Main Action.
Expanded memory Adept 8 Gains the Memory Bank trait (SW-012).
Shield emitter Master 10 Gains a personal energy shield (Light Shield, ignores Bulk, ranged attacks only).

Restraining bolts

A restraining bolt is a Light item that can be fitted to a droid as an Interaction against DS 7, or DS 10 against a droid that is aware and resisting.

  • A bolted droid cannot move more than 1 zone from the person who holds the control, and cannot make attack rolls against that person.

  • A bolted droid may try to ignore an order with a Smarts or Presence roll against DS 10. On a success it ignores that order. On a failure it obeys.

  • Removing a bolt is an Interaction with Technician tools, or any Smarts roll against DS 7.

  • A player character who is a droid may be bolted against its will only with the GM’s agreement, as part of a story.

Droids and Companions in Play

Droid soldiers and NPC droids

Droid armies use the Droid Squad rules and stat table in ERA-CW, which work with or without this module. A battle droid squad is a good first encounter for a droid player character, because it shows what the chassis is up against.

Companions

A character may have a companion by buying or building a droid, by earning a creature as a story reward, or (with FT-FORCE) by calling one with Beast Bond. A companion is controlled by the player under the GM’s guidance. The Shepard trait (CT-025) gives Advantage on all Skill Die rolls your companions make, and Dire Pet (CT-010) makes a creature companion Large and rideable. Neither trait gives you a companion by itself. It supports the character and does not replace another player.

Using companions. A companion takes its turn straight after yours, with a Movement and a Main Action. Giving a simple order is free. Giving an order that needs a roll costs your Interaction. Companions use their own stats below and the Core System for everything else.

Scaling. A companion’s HP, CD and damage rise with your level, using the pattern from the Fantasy Theme familiars. ‘Level’ below means your level.

Gear. A companion that is given a weapon stops getting the level bonus to damage. Armour on a companion applies its Armour Penalty to its CD.

Creature companions
Creature Pack hound Armoured beast Winged scavenger
HP 5 + level 12 + level 2 + level
CD 10 + level 5 + level 13 + level
Armour 0 4 0
Speed 2 zones 2 zones 3 zones
Roll to hit Player Presence Player Presence Player Presence
Damage 4 + level 6 + level 2 + level
Skill Die 1d8 1d6 1d12
Cost 60 Force Points 80 Force Points 40 Force Points

Force Points only apply when a power such as Beast Bond is used to call the creature for a time. A creature gained as a reward or by purchase stays with you and costs nothing to keep.

Droid companions
Droid Sentry droid Utility droid Medical droid
HP 6 + level 4 + level 4 + level
CD 10 + level 12 + level 11 + level
Armour 2 0 0
Speed 2 zones 2 zones 2 zones
Roll to hit Player Smarts Player Smarts Player Smarts
Damage 4 + level 1 + level None
Skill Die 1d8 1d12 1d8
Function Combat support Repair and slicing Treats allies
Cost 800 credits 600 credits 900 credits

  • Sentry droid. Once per combat, it uses its Main Action to give Advantage on the next attack roll of one ally in its zone.

  • Utility droid. Rolls its Skill Die with Advantage on repair, slicing and navigation rolls. A utility droid can act as your ship’s Engineer or Sensors operator.

  • Medical droid. Once per middle rest, it uses its Main Action to treat one ally as a medpac would, healing HP equal to your Smarts Rank + 1.

A companion may refuse, misunderstand or fail an order that conflicts with its programming, condition or abilities. The GM decides.

Commands

Command Resolution
Follow me or stay here No roll
Guard this place No roll until the situation changes
Repair this device Companion’s Smarts roll against the repair DS
Identify this target Companion’s Smarts or Senses roll
Slice this terminal Companion’s Smarts roll with Advantage if it has Droid Interface
Fight this enemy Use the companion’s combat stats
Ignore your orders Smarts or Presence roll against DS 10 (see Restraining bolts)

FT-SCUM: Bounties and Syndicates

Module card
Code FT-SCUM
Requires SW-BASE
Pairs well with ERA-GE, ERA-RT, FT-SHIPS, FT-DROIDS
Conflicts None
Overrides Nothing. Extends the Core System Bribery table.
Adds Credits and starting money, contract board, three-stage bounty hunts, quarry stats, take-alive rules, Fame and Infamy, Syndicate Standing, contacts

Smugglers, bounty hunters, pirates and mercenaries live alongside governments and legitimate trade. This module is for a crew that makes its living there: a bounty team, a smuggling run, a band of mercenaries or a group caught between two syndicates.

In this module: Credits and Contracts · Running Bounty Hunts · Reputation and the Underworld

Credits and Contracts

Credits

Credits are the galaxy’s currency and go in the Money box of the character sheet. They use the same scale as the Core System Bribery table, so 100 credits is a modest sum and 10,000 is an aristocrat’s price.

  • Starting money. Each character starts with 100 credits. The GM may raise this for a richer campaign.

  • Bribes. Use the Core System Bribery table. Paying the listed amount for the target’s lifestyle removes the -5 to the roll.

  • Shares. Unless the crew agrees otherwise, a contract’s payment is split equally between the players.

The contract board

A contract names five things before it is accepted. The GM should say all five, even when one is a lie.

Field Question
Quarry Who or what is wanted?
Client Who is paying, and why?
Reward How much, and when?
Terms Alive only, alive or dead, or a delivery condition?
Catch What is the client not saying?

A contract may be honest, misleading or a trap. A hunter with Hunter tools or Smuggler tools may roll Smarts against DS 10 to spot that a contract’s terms do not add up.

Payment

Delivery Payment
On time and as agreed Full reward
Late Reward reduced by a quarter
Injured, or in poor condition Reward reduced by half
Dead when the terms said alive Nothing
Quarry lost to a rival Nothing, and the rival may be an enemy later

Guild contracts take a 10% fee. Unguilded work has no fee, but also no protection if the client does not pay.

Contract Reward
Minor local target 100 to 500 credits
Armed criminal 500 to 1,500 credits
Experienced outlaw 1,500 to 5,000 credits
Dangerous mercenary 5,000 to 10,000 credits
Syndicate lieutenant 10,000 to 25,000 credits
Major fugitive 25,000 credits or more

Running Bounty Hunts

Bounty hunts

Run a hunt in three stages. Each stage is a scene or a short run of scenes, and the GM may skip a stage that does not need dice.

1. Locate. The crew gathers leads, pays informants, reads records and follows rumours. Roll Presence to charm or bribe a source, Smarts to read records and Senses to watch and listen. The DS is 8 for an ordinary target, 10 for a hunted one and 12 for one who is hiding well. Hunter tools give +3. A tracking beacon gives Advantage once it is on the target.

2. Track and approach. The crew finds the quarry and plans the approach. Make a Senses roll against DS 10 to track a moving target, and Agility or Presence rolls for the approach. Each success gives the crew one advantage for the final scene, such as the quarry’s guard being thin, or an exit being cut off. The GM sets a limit of 3.

3. Capture or kill. Run the fight with Zones and the Core System combat rules. Each advantage from stage 2 gives one ally Advantage on their first roll.

Quarry

Use these quick stats for the quarry and their allies. All attack rolls use Main Die plus the Skill Die shown.

Quarry HP CD AR Weapon Damage Skill Die Notes
Ordinary civilian 5 11 0 Hold-out blaster 3 1d4 Surrenders on the first hit
Local criminal 10 12 0 Blaster pistol 4 1d6 1 or 2 gang members
Experienced outlaw 15 13 1 Blaster carbine 5 1d8 Knows the local terrain
Armed mercenary 20 14 3 Blaster rifle 6 1d8 Trained in all weapons
Syndicate lieutenant 25 14 3 Heavy blaster pistol 6 1d8 2 bodyguards (use Armed mercenary)
Major syndicate figure 30 15 4 Heavy blaster pistol 6 1d12 4 bodyguards, escape route
Military fugitive 30 15 5 Blaster rifle 6 1d12 Allies in the garrison

Taking a quarry alive

Capturing a target alive is harder than killing one. When a target is dropped to 0 HP by a stun-setting blaster or a Crushing weapon, or a hunter declares non-lethal force before the hit, they are Grounded and Stunned instead of making Death Rolls.

  • Binding a target is an Interaction. A bound target is Restrained. Binder cuffs use DS 12 to break free. A net uses DS 10.

  • A target who wakes up in cuffs may try a Brawn roll to break out each round.

  • A target killed when the contract says ‘alive only’ pays nothing.

Reputation and the Underworld

Fame and Infamy

Fame and Infamy are two separate reputations tracked from 0 to 10. The GM adds a point when the crew’s acts become widely known and removes one after a long quiet period.

Score Fame Infamy
0 Unknown Unknown
1-2 Known locally Minor criminal reputation
3-4 Recognised in a region Feared by local criminals
5-6 Known across a world Notorious across a world
7-8 Known across several systems Major underworld reputation
9-10 Galaxy-wide recognition Galaxy-wide notoriety

Fame attracts allies, work and attention.

  • At 3 or more, you have +3 to Presence rolls to persuade ordinary people and to get shelter or work.

  • At 7 or more, you have Advantage on those rolls.

Infamy frightens enemies and worries everyone else.

  • At 3 or more, you have +3 to Presence rolls to intimidate.

  • At 7 or more, you have Advantage on those rolls, but Disadvantage on Presence rolls to persuade lawful NPCs and to pass checkpoints.

Crime syndicates

A syndicate controls territory, trade, people and markets. It may be an employer, an enemy or an uneasy ally.

Type Wants
Smuggling network Illegal goods and transport
Cartel Profit, influence and territory
Pirate fleet Raiding and control of routes
Mercenary guild Contracts and military work
Information broker Secrets, blackmail and intelligence
Local gang Territory, protection and street crime

Give each major syndicate four things: a leader, a goal, a resource and a weakness. This gives the crew a way to work with it, work for it, or work against it.

Syndicate Standing. Track your crew’s Standing with each syndicate from 0 to 5, in the same way as Order Standing in ERA-OR. Standing rises when you complete jobs for it and falls when you cross it.

Standing Result
0 The syndicate treats you as a threat. Its members may hunt you.
1-2 Tolerated. Work is available at normal rates.
3-4 Trusted. +3 to rolls to persuade members. Contracts pay 10% more.
5 Inner circle. Safe houses, discounts and one favour a session.
Example syndicates
Syndicate Leader Goal Resource Weakness
The Crimson Ledger (information broker) A blind archivist known only as the Auditor Own the secrets of every major house Spies in every spaceport Keeps a single paper ledger, hidden on one station
The Dust Reavers (pirate fleet) Captain Skarn Veyl Control the trade lanes of one sector Six armed freighters (use Corvette) A mutinous second-in-command
Brass Lantern Company (mercenary guild) Marshal Ottoline Kray Win a contract that makes the guild rich 60 trained soldiers Owes a large debt to a rival cartel

Underworld contacts

A contact can provide information, gear, introductions or a place to hide. A contact is not an unlimited source of favours.

Contact level Persuade DS Typical help
1: Street source 8 Rumours, a bed for the night, a cheap forged chip
2: Fixer 10 A meeting with a client, gear up to Level 3-4, a safe house
3: Boss or broker 12 A big contract, a favour from a syndicate, gear up to Level 5-6

  • A contact is persuaded with a Presence roll against the DS. Paying the Bribery table amount for their lifestyle removes the -5 penalty.

  • A favour owed to a contact must be repaid. Breaking a deal costs 1 Syndicate Standing with that contact’s group and may add 1 Infamy.

Heat

Heat measures how much attention the law is paying you. With ERA-GE on, use the Heat track in that module. With any other Era, track Local Heat for each world or sector using the same table. Heat is separate from Fame and Infamy: a crew may be famous and unwanted, or unknown and hunted.

APP-CONVERT: Fantasy-to-Star-Wars Conversion Sheet

Module card
Code APP-CONVERT
Requires None
Pairs well with Any module
Conflicts None
Overrides None
Adds Cross reference from Fantasy Theme content to Star Wars equivalents

After World is built to be re-flavoured. Use this sheet to bring Fantasy Theme content into a Star Wars game, or to take Star Wars content back to the Fantasy Theme, without rebuilding the mechanics. Change the description and keep the profile.

In this module: Using the Conversion Guide · Equipment · Characters · Abilities and Powers · Creatures

Using the Conversion Guide

Ask three questions of anything you convert:

  • Does the original mechanic still make sense?

  • Can the description change without changing the mechanic?

  • Does the conversion give one character an advantage that others cannot reasonably get?

If the first two answers are yes and the third is no, convert it. If not, ask your GM or give it a new profile.

Equipment

Weapons

Fantasy Star Wars Profile
Dagger Combat knife or vibro-knife Light Melee
Shortsword Vibroblade Medium Melee
Longsword Vibroblade Medium Melee
Axe Vibro-axe Medium Melee
Mace or club Gaderffii or stun baton Light or Medium Melee
Greatsword Vibrosword Heavy Melee
Spear or pike Force pike Heavy Melee, Reach
Staff Electrostaff Heavy Melee
Sling or thrown knife Hold-out blaster Light Ranged
Shortbow Blaster pistol or carbine Light or Medium Ranged
Crossbow Bowcaster Heavy Ranged, Piercing, Loading
Longbow Blaster rifle or sniper rifle Heavy Ranged
Ballista or catapult Repeating blaster cannon Siege
Alchemist’s fire Flame projector or thermal detonator Burning or Blast Thrown

Keep the original profile, damage and tags unless the Star Wars item has a stat block in SW-BASE, in which case use that.

Do not make a new armour statistic because a description is futuristic. Pick the closest Armour Profile.

Characters

Species and races

The Fantasy races are not converted one for one. Use a Star Wars species in SW-BASE that matches the idea, or build one from the Offworld Born or Custom Species variants. As examples:

Fantasy race Star Wars option
Damar (diplomatic, Senator trait) Human or Bothan with the Senator trait (Offworld Born)
Celisen (desert nomads, agile) Human from a desert world with Rank 1 Agility and Disarm (Offworld Born)
Beast-folk Wookiee, Trandoshan, Gamorrean, Whiphid or a Custom Species
Constructed beings A droid frame (FT-DROIDS)
Elf-kin Kel Dor, Togruta, Mirialan, Chiss or a Custom Species

Backgrounds

Fantasy Star Wars
Soldier Soldier
Rogue Smuggler
Hunter Bounty Hunter
Scholar Mystic or Diplomat
Healer Medic
Craftsman Technician
Sailor Spacer
Noble Diplomat

Traits

Most traits convert unchanged. A trait for a bow may apply to a blaster if the GM agrees the weapon type matches. Traits that depend on magic, the undead or fantasy assumptions need the GM’s approval, and FT-FORCE says which ones work.

Abilities and Powers

Magic and the Force

If FT-FORCE is on, convert a spell by its effect, not its name. Healing becomes Force Heal, an object moved by magic becomes Telekinesis, and so on. The full one-to-one list of database spells is in FT-FORCE.

If FT-FORCE is off, do not turn spells into Force powers. Use equipment, a trait, a stim or a story effect instead.

Fantasy Star Wars
Magic points Force Points
Angelic magic Light powers
Demonic magic Dark powers
Sword Forms Lightsaber forms
Alchemy Chemistry and Medicine
Summoning, familiars Companions (FT-DROIDS)
Vampirism A Dark-side parasite. Treat 1 feed as a Force Drain use and keep the Fantasy tables.
Lycanthropy A mutagen or rage curse. Keep the Fantasy tables.

Creatures

Keep the creature’s role and difficulty. A dangerous creature should still be dangerous after conversion.

Fantasy creature Star Wars version
Wolf Alien pack predator
Bear Armoured beast
Giant spider Native hive predator
Dragon Large flying reptile or a colossal beast
Goblin Small alien scavenger
Troll Regenerating creature
Undead Reanimated organism, experiment or ruined droid
Elemental A storm, an ion field or a failing reactor

Gamemaster Guidance

In this module: Adventure hooks · Preparing a Campaign · Running the Campaign · Encounters and Progression

Adventure hooks

Preset Hook
Jedi Knight A sealed temple has opened on a quiet world, and both orders have sent someone to claim what is inside.
Generals’ War A clone company is cut off behind enemy lines and the only way out is through a droid-held fortress.
Rebellion A defecting officer offers a list of safe houses, but one of the stops is a trap.
Scum and Villainy A cartel hires the crew to deliver a sealed crate, and a rival offers triple to lose it.
Street Level A curfew has locked down a city district. A medic, a smuggler and a guard have until dawn to cross it.
Hidden Force A child on a border world has begun to move things with their mind, and an Inquisitor is on the way.

Preparing a Campaign

Building a campaign

Decide what kind of story you want, then pick the modules. A Jedi story needs different rules from a smuggler story.

  • Start with SW-BASE, pick one Era, then pick the Features that support the story.

  • Do not turn on every module. A short list is easier to learn and to run.

  • Tell the players what is on. Player options come only from modules that are switched on.

Setting the tone

Explain the tone before the first session. Some options:

Tone The story is about
Heroic adventure Joining great events and fighting for a cause
Military drama Serving in a unit and living with the cost of war
Underworld adventure Smugglers, hunters and mercenaries working for credits
Political drama Governments, houses and competing interests
Survival Getting through dangerous worlds alive
Personal story Individual goals and relationships against the galaxy

Say how common the Force is, how advanced the technology is and which factions matter.

Running the Campaign

Difficulty Scores

Use the Core System’s advice on setting a DS. If you want a starting point, these values fit the numbers already in this Theme.

Task DS
Easy 8
Standard 10
Hard 12
Heroic 14
Legendary 16

Examples from this Theme: Stun setting DS 11, Checkpoint DS 10 + Heat, Hyperspace DS 8 to 14, Force DS 10 + Senses or Presence Rank.

Running factions

A faction has goals of its own that do not depend on the players. It wants territory, money, influence, freedom, safety or revenge, and its members act on that. Players should be able to bargain with a faction, work for it, fight it or change its mind.

Running the Force

  • Common Force. Anyone can take it. Good for Jedi and Sith stories.

  • Rare Force. Only Jedi Padawan, Sith Acolyte or Mystic characters can take it. Good for a Rebellion with a secret.

  • Hidden Force. Use the Hidden Force preset. Using a power in public adds Heat.

Do not let a single Force user overshadow the table. If they do, use the Tiered Force option in FT-FORCE, or give the others traits and gear that matter.

Running droids

Droids as player characters give you a character who is almost always at the table. Give their limitations real weight, and let them find ways around them. For a droid companion, give the player the personality and the GM the veto.

Swapping Eras between acts

A campaign that spans more than one Era should use one Era module at a time. When the story jumps, swap the module and the factions with it. Keep the characters and their traits. Heat, War Effort, Exposure and Order Standing do not carry over.

Encounters and Progression

Rewards and progression

Characters level up using the Core System. New gear should support a character, not make their traits pointless. Give out loot at the pace the story needs, not at the pace the tables suggest.

Adversaries

Statistics for Star Wars adversaries, both people and creatures, are in the Star Wars Catalogue in Part VI, the Enemy Compendium. Use the Droid Squad rules in ERA-CW for groups of droids and trooper squads.

APP-LOOT: Loot Tables

Module card
Code APP-LOOT
Requires SW-BASE
Pairs well with Any Era or Feature
Conflicts None
Overrides None
Adds Loot tiers for levels 1 to 7, credit rewards, location tables, salvage and price guide

Loot should fit the place and the enemy. A garrison holds military supplies and a smuggler’s den holds contraband. These tables are a guide, and the GM does not have to roll for every item.

In this module: Using Loot · Rewards and Item Tiers · Location Loot · Salvage and crafting · Price guide

Using Loot

  1. Pick a location table (or the General table if none fits) and roll 1d10.

  2. Use the tier table that matches the party’s level to turn the result into a specific item.

  3. If the item is a consumable or small gear, give 1 to 3 of them. If it is a weapon or armour, give one.

7 A sealed sample that someone will pay for
8 Prosthetic limb
9 A prototype stim (use the tier above)
10 An experiment that is still running

Ruin or temple

1d10 Discovery
1 Old coins worth 50 x tier credits
2 A carved idol or relic worth 100 x tier credits
3 A data crystal with a star chart
4 A holocron fragment (Advantage on one Smarts roll, once)
5 An ancient weapon (vibroblade or vibrosword)
6 A scroll of rituals (Mystic tools +3 for one study)
7 A trapped chest (Smarts DS 10 to open safely)
8 A kyber crystal (FT-FORCE) or a sealed relic (ERA-OR)
9 A hidden passage
10 A relic that is also a curse (GM)

Wreck or junk heap

1d10 Discovery
1 2 power cells
2 10 item materials (Novice)
3 Scrap armour (Light Armour)
4 A broken blaster (Novice repair, DS 7)
5 A working comlink
6 5 item materials (Adept)
7 A salvaged droid part (Upgrade for the tier)
8 A flight recorder with a clue
9 Fuel, rations and spare parts
10 A hidden cargo or a droid that still works

Salvage and crafting

Recovered materials can be used with the crafting rules. A successful roll does not mean the character can make anything. The materials, tools, time and knowledge must still fit.

Skill level DS Salvage needed
Novice 7 Scrap, plastoid, synthetic fibre
Adept 8 Durasteel, alloys, fine circuitry. Needs a workshop.
Master 10 Rare alloys, crystals, prototype cores. Needs a lab or factory.

Price guide

Use these as a guide for buying and selling. Sellers usually pay a third to half of these prices.

Item Typical price
Tier 1 consumable 25 to 150 credits
Tier 2 consumable 200 to 800 credits
Tier 3 consumable 1,000 to 3,000 credits
Tier 4 consumable 5,000 credits or more
Light weapon 100 to 300 credits
Medium weapon 300 to 800 credits
Heavy weapon 800 to 2,000 credits
Light or Reinforced armour 200 to 600 credits
Balanced or Heavy armour 800 to 2,500 credits
Full armour or Powered armour 3,000 to 10,000 credits
Droid companion 600 to 900 credits
Light freighter 20,000 credits or more

Core rules reminder

Rule Value
Main Die 1d10 on every roll. A 10 is a critical success and a 1 is a critical failure.
Skill Dice Rank 1 = 1d4, Rank 2 = 1d6, Rank 3 = 1d8, Rank 4 = 1d12
Attributes Brawn, Agility, Health, Smarts, Senses, Presence
HP Health Rank x 5
Combat Defence 12 + Agility Rank, less any Armour Penalty
Carry weight Brawn Rank x 5. Light 1, Medium 3, Heavy 6.
Rests Light 1 hour, middle 4 hours, full 8 hours
Death Rolls 1d6 at 0 HP. Above 3 passes, below 3 fails, a 3 rerolls. Roll 3 times: more passes and you rise on 1 HP, more fails and you die.
Wounds Maximum 3. They cost 1, 2 and 3 ranks from every attribute. A fourth is death.

Essential Core Rules

A short reminder of the Core System rules used in every game. Full rules are in Part I.

Rule Summary
A roll Roll the d10. A 10 succeeds and a 1 fails. Otherwise add your Skill Die and bonuses and beat the target: Combat Defence for attacks, a DS for everything else.
Your turn Main action (attack or cast), Movement, Interaction, and Opportunity or Surprise Actions when they apply.
Damage An attack that beats CD deals weapon damage plus bonuses (double on a critical). Subtract Armour Rating, but never below half (rounded up). Subtract the result from HP.
Dying At 0 HP roll a d6 three times. Above 3 passes, below 3 fails, a 3 rerolls. More passes means you rise on 1 HP, and more fails means death.
Wounds Take one to turn a hit into 0 damage. Maximum 3. They cost 1, 2 and 3 ranks from every attribute.
Rests Light rest 1 hour, middle rest 4 hours, full rest 8 hours.

How to Read an Entry

The Ability Database is organised by system: Core System, Fantasy, Modern and Star Wars. Inside each system, abilities are grouped into families, such as Core Traits or Universal Spells. Abilities that every Theme can use are in the Core System. Abilities that belong to one Theme are in that Theme’s system, and an ability appears in full only once.

Every entry has the same layout: a code and name, a table of its details, and then its description. Only the details an ability actually has are shown.

Detail Meaning
System The system the ability belongs to.
Category The family or kind of ability.
Ranked Whether the ability can be taken more than once to improve it.
Prerequisite What you need before you can take it.
Range, Cost, Duration, Uses, Action, Trigger When and how the ability can be used and what it costs.
Damage, Damage Scaling, Secrets Scaling How the ability grows with level.
Weakness A drawback built into the ability.
Used by Which systems use the ability, where this is more than one.

Ability codes, such as CT-001 or AS-002, never change. Use the Master Alphabetical Index to find any ability by name.

Master Alphabetical Index

Find any ability by name. Click a name to go to its entry.

CT-001 — A Fine Addition

System Core System
Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Used by All Themes

You gain +50 Carry Weight, but only for weapons.

CT-002 — Adrenaline Rush

System Core System
Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Used by All Themes

When you kill an enemy with a melee attack, your movement is doubled until the end of your turn.

CT-003 — Angel Outlaw

System Core System
Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Used by All Themes

For every 10 Fame, you gain +1 to rolls that persuade.

For every 10 Infamy, you gain +1 to rolls that intimidate.

Neither bonus can exceed +5.

CT-004 — Attractive Intelligence

System Core System
Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Used by All Themes

You may use Smarts instead of Presence when trying to seduce someone.

CT-005 — Bulwark

System Core System
Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Used by All Themes

Your Armour Rating increases by +2, but you cannot move while this is active.

CT-006 — Close The Distance

System Core System
Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Uses Once per Combat
Used by All Themes

You move to the edge of one zone, in any direction you can see, as a Free Action.

CT-007 — Crafter

System Core System
Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked Yes
Prerequisite None
Used by All Themes

Applies to rolls for crafting.

Rank 1 — No Disadvantage on crafting rolls. Rank 2 — Gain Advantage on crafting rolls. Rank 3 — Gain double Skill Dice on crafting rolls.

CT-008 — Criminal Scum

System Core System
Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked Yes
Prerequisite None
Used by All Themes

Applies to rolls for lockpicking.

Rank 1 — No Disadvantage on lockpicking rolls. Rank 2 — Gain Advantage on lockpicking rolls. Rank 3 — Gain double Skill Dice on lockpicking rolls.

CT-009 — Deflecting Dance

System Core System
Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Uses Once per Combat
Used by All Themes

You may block an incoming melee attack as a Free Action, reducing the damage by 1d8.

If the damage is reduced to 0, you gain an Opportunity Action.

CT-010 — Dire Pet

System Core System
Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Used by All Themes

Your familiar grows into a larger version and can now be mounted.

It now has Large movement and land speed.

System Core System
Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked Yes
Prerequisite None

Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Used by All Themes

When you hit an armoured enemy, you may choose to damage their armour instead of dealing damage. If you do, you deal half your weapon’s normal damage to the armour instead of the enemy.

Damage to armour is determined by your weapon’s effects against enemy armour.

Used by All Themes

When a projectile would hit you, you may use an Interaction to reduce the damage by your Armour Rating + 3.

If reduced to 0, the projectile is broken and you keep your Interaction.

System Core System
Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked No

You gain +2 to Agility rolls when trying to hide yourself.

CT-030 — Strider

System Core System
Category Core Trait (Utility)
Ranked No
Prerequisite Handling a light or medium melee weapon.
Used by All Themes

When a projectile would hit you, you may use an Interaction to roll 1d8 against the attack’s damage.

If your roll is higher, the projectile is deflected or knocked away.

Category Advanced Combat Trait
Ranked No

Choose one type of armour. While wearing that type of armour, you gain +1 Armour Rating.

AC-002 — Black Belt

System Core System
Category Advanced Combat Trait
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Used by All Themes

Your unarmed attacks use Agility for Attack Rolls.

Your unarmed attacks deal 3 damage.

AC-003 — Borrow

System Core System
Category Advanced Combat Trait
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Duration 1 Turn
Uses Once per Combat
Used by All Themes

Gain the benefits of another character’s Core Combat Trait for 1 turn. You must still meet all prerequisites.

AC-004 — Commanding Concussion

System Core System
Category Advanced Combat Trait
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Uses Once per Combat
Action Opportunity Action
Trigger A creature attacks within your melee reach.
Used by All Themes

The attacking creature must make a Health Roll.

On a failure, it loses its next turn.

AC-005 — Disarm

System Core System
Category Advanced Combat Trait
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Uses Once per Combat
Trigger Critical Hit
Used by All Themes

Instead of applying the normal effects of your Critical Hit, you may destroy the target’s weapon.

AC-006 — Distracting Presence

System Core System
Category Advanced Combat Trait
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Range 1 Zone
Action Interaction
Used by All Themes

Choose any number of creatures within 1 Zone.

Each creature must make a Presence Roll against DS 10 + half your Presence Dice.

Creatures that fail have Disadvantage on attacks that do not target you until the end of the turn.

AC-007 — Evasive Action

System Core System
Category Advanced Combat Trait
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Used by All Themes

Until the end of your turn, Opportunity Actions made against your movement are rolled with Disadvantage.

AC-008 — Feint

System Core System
Category Advanced Combat Trait
Ranked No
Prerequisite Must be able to make two attacks with a Full Action.
Used by All Themes

You may intentionally miss your first attack.

If you do, your second attack automatically hits.

AC-009 — Healthy

System Core System
Category Advanced Combat Trait
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Used by All Themes

Add your Health Dice to your maximum Health one additional time.

Increase your maximum Health by 1 for every level after Level 1.

AC-010 — Hitman

System Core System
Category Advanced Combat Trait
Ranked No
Prerequisite None
Used by All Themes

Whenever you hit with a Sneak Attack, the attack deals Critical Damage.

AC-011 — If It Works, It Works

System Core System
Category Advanced Combat Trait
Ranked No
Prerequisite Using an improvised object.
Used by All Themes

Improvised objects deal damage based on size.

  • Light: 3 Damage

  • Medium: 5 Damage

  • Heavy: 7 Damage

Category Advanced Combat Trait

System Core System

Ranked No

Used by All Themes

MT-001 — Rampage

System Core System
Category Melee Trait
Ranked Yes
Prerequisite 1 Wound taken
Action Free Action to enter, Once per Combat
Used by All Themes

While Rampaging:

Rank 1 — +3 Damage with melee attacks.

Rank 2 — +6 Damage with melee attacks. Your Combat Defence is reduced by 2. You cannot use Interactions to disengage, retreat, or use non-offensive items.

Rank 3 — +9 Damage with melee attacks. Your Combat Defence is reduced by 3. You must attack the nearest creature you can reach each turn, friend or foe.

Rampage ends when you choose, when combat ends, or when you are reduced to 0 Health.

System Core System
Category Melee Trait
Ranked No
Prerequisite Using a medium or heavy melee weapon.
Used by All Themes

MT-012 — Momentum

System Core System
Category Melee Trait
Ranked No
Prerequisite Using a melee weapon.
Used by All Themes

Each consecutive turn you hit the same creature with a melee attack, gain +1 Damage against that creature.

This stacks up to a maximum of +3.

The bonus resets if you miss, switch targets, or the creature is defeated.

Used by All Themes

System Fantasy

Ranked Yes
Prerequisite None
Used by Fantasy

Rank 1 — All spells except Augment Strength and Speed cost 5 less magic at all levels. Rank 2 — Spell cost decrease is 10. Rank 3 — Spell cost decrease is 15.

This discount can never reduce a spell’s cost below one quarter of its listed base cost.

Modern Abilities

Abilities that belong to the Modern Theme.

Superpowers

All powers use Tiers 1–3 with no point costs. When chosen, all powers start at Tier 1 and go up every time it is picked during a level up. This requires giving up a trait.

You can Over Channel (Tier 4+) to push beyond normal limits; each tier above 3 inflicts 1 Fatigue.

Attacks use 1d10 + Attribute Skill Die vs the target’s Combat Defence. Resisted effects make the target roll vs your Power Defence.

SP-001 — Absorption

System Modern
Category Superpower
Weakness Absorbing opposing energies (Heat/Cold) causes backlash.
Used by Modern

Absorb energy or material to gain traits.

Tier 1 — Absorb 1 elemental type (Heat, Cold, Lightning); gain Resistance for 1 round. Tier 2 — Store absorbed energy; next attack deals +2 Damage of that type. Tier 3 — Transform partially; gain +2 AR and +2 Damage for 1 round. Tier 4 — Fully transform; gain immunity to that element and deal +4 Damage. Tier 5 — Absorb all elemental energy in zone; massive 15 Damage burst. Fatigue per extra tier.

SP-002 — Animalistic Mutation (Movement)

System Modern
Category Superpower
Weakness Animal instincts may override judgment.
Used by Modern

Gain traits of animals (Toad, Spider, etc.).

Tier 1 — Gain Climb, Jump, or Swim traits; +2 to related rolls. Tier 2 — Gain natural weapons (4 Damage) and +1 zone movement. Tier 3 — Gain 8 Damage melee strike and Advantage on Acrobatics. Tier 4 — Gain +3 AR and Regeneration (4 HP/turn). Tier 5 — Beast form: +10 Damage, +2 zones movement. Fatigue per extra tier.

SP-003 — Drain

System Modern
Category Superpower
Weakness Prolonged drain causes memory bleed or identity confusion.
Used by Modern

Absorb vitality or powers from others.

Tier 1 — Touch drains 4 HP and heals you for half that amount (round up). Tier 2 — Drain 6 HP and 1 Tier 1 power for 1 round (target rolls vs your Power Defence). Tier 3 — Drain 9 HP and a Tier 2 power for 1 round. Tier 4 — Drain 12 HP and multiple powers. Tier 5 — Completely drain one target (unconscious). Fatigue per extra tier.

SP-004 — Duplication

System Modern
Category Superpower
Weakness Linked pain; duplicates vanish if you are incapacitated.
Used by Modern

Create autonomous duplicates.

Tier 1 — Create 1 duplicate (HP = ⅓ yours, Damage = 2, no powers). Acts on your initiative. Tier 2 — Create 2 duplicates with the same stats. Tier 3 — Create 3 duplicates with the same stats. Tier 4 — 4 duplicates. Tier 5 — 6 duplicates. If a duplicate dies, you take 2 damage. Fatigue per extra tier.

SP-005 — Electrokinesis

System Modern
Category Superpower
Used by Modern

Command and project lightning.

Tier 1 — 4 Lightning Damage, single target. Roll: 1d10 + Senses vs Combat Defence. Tier 2 — 4 Lightning Damage, Burst (target zone). Resist: targets roll vs your Power Defence; on fail, also disrupt (no reload/Interaction next turn). Tier 3 — 6 Lightning Damage, Burst (target zone); on failed resist, electronics disabled until repaired. Tier 4 — 10 Damage, Burst (2 zones); on failed resist, stunned 1 round. Tier 5 — 13 Damage, chain to adjacent zone. Fatigue per extra tier.

SP-006 — Enhanced Senses

System Modern
Category Superpower
Weakness Sensory overload possible in chaos.
Used by Modern

Superhuman awareness and perception.

Tier 1 — Advantage on Senses rolls; detect hidden enemies in your zone. Tier 2 — See through walls/thin barriers; ignore Disadvantage from darkness or cover. Tier 3 — Detect weak points; your next attack Ignores Armour. Tier 4 — Perceive all zones; no hidden creatures. Tier 5 — Predict movements; all attacks at Advantage for 1 round. Fatigue per extra tier.

SP-007 — Flight (Movement)

System Modern
Category Superpower
Used by Modern

Controlled aerial movement.

Tier 1 — Fly 1 zone; disengage freely when you start flying this turn. Tier 2 — Fly 2 zones; Advantage on checks to avoid aerial hazards. Tier 3 — Fly 3 zones; may carry 1 Medium ally. Tier 4 — Fly 4 zones and take the Outer Zone route, ignoring obstacles. Tier 5 — 5 zones; act before ground-bound enemies this round. Fatigue per extra tier.

SP-008 — Freeze Breath

System Modern
Category Superpower
Used by Modern

Supercooled exhalation that immobilizes.

Tier 1 — 2 Cold Damage, Cone (your zone); targets roll vs your Power Defence or Slowed (speed 0) until your next turn. Tier 2 — 4 Cold Damage, Cone; on fail, Restrained until your next turn (breaks if they take Heat damage). Tier 3 — 6 Cold Damage, Cone; on fail, Frozen (no Main action) until your next turn. Tier 4 — 9 Damage, Burst (target zone); on fail, encased (cannot move or act) until damaged. Tier 5 — 12 Damage, create Ice Wall sealing zone edges. Fatigue per extra tier.

Appendix: Design Notes and Changelog

Everything from here on is commentary: why each rebalance pass was made, what was deliberately left alone, and what is flagged for a future look. None of it is rules text.

Rebalance Changelog (v1.2)

Design goal for this pass: keep challenge present at every level, keep players feeling powerful, and keep the baseline of play cooldown-free — the few effects that do carry a Fatigue cost are the extreme top-tier ones, and a character always has a free fallback (a lower tier of the same power, a weapon, another trait) available the same turn. Nothing below was deleted; every change is a numeric or conditional tweak to an existing entry.

Traits

  • AC-001 Armour Mastery — bonus reduced from +2 to +1 Armour Rating. At +2, stacking this with a Full/Powered Armour profile (base AR 5) reached AR 7, which fully blocked every enemy in the Enemies book except the Greater Monster (and even that only took 2 damage through). At +1, top-end armour reaches AR 6 — it still trivializes the lowest-level enemies (intended, since those are meant to be easy fights by the level a character affords this combo) but leaves Tyrant/Monster/Greater Monster still landing real damage.

  • RT-004 Longshot — trigger changed from “2 Zones away” (already the standard max range for most ranged weapons, making the bonus effectively always-on) to “at the maximum range of your weapon.” Same +4 Damage, but now a positioning reward tied to your specific weapon’s range rather than a passive freebie.

  • CT-003 Angel/Outlaw — capped at +5 per bonus (50 Fame / 50 Infamy). Previously uncapped, so it could keep climbing for the rest of a campaign and eventually outscale every other social bonus in the database.

  • CT-020 Like Butter — the armour-damage option now deals half your weapon’s normal damage instead of full damage. Previously it was a strictly free choice between two full-value outcomes with no tradeoff.

  • FT-006 Unlimited Power — added a floor: the discount can’t take a spell below one quarter of its listed base cost. Previously a Rank 3 character could stack the flat -15 discount against cheap spells and get them for free or less.

  • MT-007 Bloodied Frenzy — added an end condition: the half-damage-taken defensive effect now also ends if you climb back above half Health, not just at combat end or 0 HP. Previously, once triggered, the defensive half-damage stuck around for the rest of the fight even after healing back to full.

Spells

  • US-003 Blast, US-013 Orb, US-018 Telekinesis — all three Universal cantrips now cost 10 magic instead of being free. They were dealing 5-6 damage (on par with a Medium Melee weapon) at zero resource cost, which made every other spell in the game — including the two Demonic damage spells below — look overpriced by comparison. 10 magic keeps them cheap and spammable relative to a magic pool that refills on a light rest, without being literally free.

  • DS-002 Choke, DS-004 Fireball — base cost reduced from 80 to 50 (per-level/per-3-levels scaling also reduced from +50 to +35). These were costing roughly 13x a free Universal cantrip for about 2x the damage; the new cost brings their damage-per-magic ratio up to line up with Lightning, the best-priced Demonic damage spell.

  • DS-008 Screech — base cost reduced from 50 to 35, with the same proportional reduction to its scaling, for the same reason as Choke and Fireball.

Superpowers

  • SP-005 Electrokinesis, SP-008 Freeze Breath, SP-018 Poison Production, SP-022 Sonic Scream, SP-032 Weather Control — Tier 4 damage raised (8→9 or 8→10) and Tier 5 raised (10→12 or 10→13). These five are the AoE/control-focused powers that capped well below the single-target damage powers (15 at Tier 5) even though the AoE tax was already being paid via lower per-target numbers. The gap is narrowed, not eliminated — they still sit below the pure single-target group, which is an intentional AoE tradeoff, just a smaller one.

  • SP-031 Weapon Summoning — Tier 4/5 raised from 10/12 to 12/15, bringing its ceiling up to match the other single-target damage powers (Kinetic Charge, Metalkinesis, Pyrokinesis, etc.), which it had fallen behind despite a similar shape.

  • SP-027 Telepathy — damage progression smoothed from 3/3/4/3/0 to 3/4/5/6/(Mind Stop). The old curve barely moved with tier investment and actually dropped at Tier 4 before the Tier 5 finisher replaced damage with unconsciousness entirely; it now climbs steadily like every other power’s damage column.

  • SP-003 Drain — self-heal changed from full HP drained to half HP drained (round up). Drain was the only power in the database where every point of damage also fully healed the caster — strong enough to be a de facto better version of a pure damage power. It’s still a strong niche (guaranteed sustain on a hit) at half value instead of double value.

Left alone on purpose

  • DS-001 Blood Spear (free, 4 damage) — already the Demonic equivalent of the free Universal cantrips, and was the reference point used to reprice Choke, Fireball, and Screech. No change needed.

  • SP-010 Heat Vision’s free Ignores Armour at Tier 1 — looked like an outlier in isolation, but every other single-target power already gets its own non-damage rider somewhere in its curve (Metalkinesis disarms, Terrakinesis/Super Strength push or shove, Telekinesis gains Ignores Armour at Tier 3, Pyrokinesis ignites terrain). Heat Vision just gets its rider earlier. No change made.

  • SP-004 Duplication, SP-023 Super Speed, and every other power’s Tier 4-5 numbers — these already carry the system-wide Over Channel cost (1 Fatigue per tier above 3, so Tier 4 = 1 Fatigue, Tier 5 = 2 Fatigue) baked into the Core System’s power rules. That existing cost already does the job of gating the strongest effects behind a resource, while leaving Tiers 1-3 completely free — which is exactly the “no cooldown, or a fallback if there is one” shape asked for. No separate fix needed.

Outside this document — a Core System note, not a trait fix

The Armour Rating stacking issue traced back to more than just Armour Mastery: Full/Powered Armour alone already provides AR 5, which by itself blocks the two lowest-level enemies outright and heavily reduces everything else, since the Core System treats Armour Rating as a hard block-threshold rather than a percentage reduction. Nerfing Armour Mastery (above) pulls the worst-case combo back into a reasonable range, but if separately-stacked AR bonuses become a recurring problem as more content is added, the more durable fix would be a total-AR cap in the Core System itself (e.g. “Armour Rating from all sources combined cannot exceed 8”), rather than nerfing every individual trait or item that adds AR one at a time. Flagging this for awareness — no change was made to the Core System file itself.

Rebalance Changelog (v1.3)

MT-001 Frenzy — full redesign, merged with MT-007

  • MT-001 Frenzy is now a Ranked trait (Rank 1/2/3, unlocked the same way as every other Ranked trait — re-picking it with a later trait pick), replacing the old flat “+2 Damage, +4 below half Health” version.

  • Rank 1: +3 Damage with melee attacks, Combat Defence -2.

  • Rank 2: +6 Damage, Combat Defence -3, can’t use Interactions to disengage, retreat, or use non-offensive items while frenzied.

  • Rank 3: +9 Damage, Combat Defence -4, must attack the nearest creature you can reach each turn — friend or foe.

  • At the end of every 3rd consecutive turn frenzied, take 1 Wound.

  • Once per Combat, ends on choice, combat end, or 0 Health.

  • MT-007 Bloodied Frenzy is superseded by the above — its card now points to MT-001 and is marked so no one takes it going forward, rather than being deleted outright.

  • Why the redesign: the old pair paid for their power almost entirely with a delayed cost (a damage bonus that only kicked in near death) and, in Bloodied Frenzy’s case, a defensive half-damage effect that could persist even after healing back to full — see the v1.2 Bloodied Frenzy entry above for that specific fix, which is now moot since the trait it patched no longer exists in this form. The new version pays for its power every single turn it’s active (lower Combat Defence, loss of tactical options, forced targeting at Rank 3) plus a real, countable long-fight cost (recurring Wounds), rather than a one-time gate or a bill that arrives after the danger has passed. It’s also now priced against its own opportunity cost properly — reaching Rank 3 costs three full trait picks, the same currency as maxing Weapon Mastery, so its ceiling (+9 flat across all melee weapons) was set high enough to be worth that investment on its own, without needing the old prerequisite chain between two separate cards.

Rebalance Changelog (v1.4)

MT-001 — finalized as Rampage, replacing the “****Frenzy****”** draft from v1.3**

Design settled on the following, locked in as final:

  • Renamed Frenzy → Rampage.

  • Prerequisite added: 1 Wound taken (ties activation to the Wound system instead of a flat HP percentage).

  • Rank 1 is now cost-free: +3 melee damage with no Combat Defence penalty or restriction. The danger of Rampage now arrives at Rank 2, not Rank 1 — a deliberate choice to give the trait a “light taste” at minimum investment.

  • Rank 2/3 penalties reduced by 1 from the v1.3 draft: Combat Defence -2/-3 (was -3/-4), since Rank 1 no longer contributes a penalty of its own and the total risk across the three ranks was rebalanced around that.

  • Recurring Wound-per-3-turns clause removed. v1.3’s “1 Wound every 3rd turn” is gone — Rampage’s cost is now fixed per-rank (Combat Defence and lost options) rather than escalating the longer a fight runs. This is a real simplification, not just a rename: a Rank 3 character can now sustain Rampage for an entire fight at a flat cost, with no scaling penalty for staying in it longer. Noted here in case a long-fight throttle is wanted back in later — the recurring-Wound clause from v1.3 is the natural thing to reintroduce if Rampage turns out too sustainable in play.

MT-007’s card now points to “Rampage” by name.

New Content (v1.5) — Four New Melee Traits

Added to fill gaps the Melee Trait list didn’t cover — nothing rewarded standing your ground defensively, nothing hit more than one target per swing, nothing rewarded sustained focus-fire without a drawback, and nothing gave a clean way to Grapple.

MT-010 — Riposte — Opportunity Action, once per turn: punish a missed attack against you with a free melee attack. The defensive counterpart to Rampage — rewards a high-Combat-Defence build instead of trading Defence away.

MT-011 — Cleave — on a hit with a medium/heavy weapon, also hit a second creature in reach for half damage. The one genuinely new shape in the list — nothing else hits more than one target in a single melee swing (Fast Attacks and That Still Only Counts As One both hit the same target twice, not two different ones).

MT-012 — Momentum — stacking +1 Damage (max +3) for consecutive turns hitting the same target, no drawback attached. A deliberately “clean” pick — Rampage, Backstab, and Devastating Blow are all conditional or risky in their own way; this gives players who don’t want that shape a straightforward reward for focus-firing a target.

MT-013 — Grappler — on a hit with an unarmed strike or light weapon, Grapple instead of dealing damage. Fills the gap for a non-damage-focused melee build; Grappled already existed as a status in the Core System’s Effects table, but nothing in the trait list granted an easy way to inflict it without a separate contested roll.

Rebalance Changelog (v1.6)

These changes are present in the v1.6 document but were never written up. They are recorded here from a line-by-line comparison against v1.1. The entries and numbers below are accurate; the reasoning behind them was not captured at the time and has not been invented here.

Traits

  • FT-002 Control Animal (Vampire) — Cost changed from “1 Feed” to “3 Blood Points (+3 every 3 levels)”.

Spells

  • AS-001 Absorb Magic — Cost added: 15 + 10 every 3 levels. The entry previously carried no Cost line at all.

  • DS-003 Drain Life — damage now equals the enemy’s Level rather than their Health Rank, and the self-heal is half that amount (round up) rather than the full amount. This is the same change v1.2 made to SP-003 Drain, applied to the Demonic equivalent.

Superpowers

  • SP-002 Animalistic Mutation — Tier 4 Armour Rating bonus reduced from 8 AR to +3 AR.

  • SP-009 Healing Factor — Tier 1 restricted to self (was “self or touch”). Weakness extended with “Damage is still felt.”

  • SP-013 Invulnerability — Tier 4 reduced from +8 AR to +6 AR; Tier 5 reduced from +10 AR to +7 AR, with a new rider added: the first magic or superpower hit against you each round is halved.

Observation carried forward

SP-002 and SP-013 both pull large flat Armour Rating grants down into a much tighter band, which is the direction the v1.2 note on AR stacking pointed. If a total-AR cap is ever added to the Core System, these two entries and AC-001 Armour Mastery are the ones to re-check against it.

Merge Changelog (v1.7)

v1.7 merges the v1.6 document and the v1.1 document into a single file and restores the design notes, which v1.6 had dropped. No ability was rebalanced as part of this merge — the only rules change is the deletion of MT-007, below.

Source of truth

  • v1.1 supplied MT-001 Rampage and the four v1.5 melee traits — MT-010 Riposte, MT-011 Cleave, MT-012 Momentum and MT-013 Grappler — none of which had reached the v1.6 document despite its higher version number. v1.6 appears to have branched before v1.5 was written.

  • v1.6 supplied everything else, including the six undocumented changes now written up in the v1.6 changelog above.

  • Where the two files differed only in layout — bullet characters, field labels on their own line versus inline — v1.6’s inline layout was used throughout.

MT-007 Bloodied Frenzy — deleted

MT-007 is removed from the database entirely. v1.3 chose to retain its card as a pointer to MT-001 so that nobody would take it going forward, but v1.6 never applied that and still carried the trait live, with a prerequisite (“Has the Frenzy Trait”) that stopped pointing at anything once MT-001 was renamed to Rampage. Rather than keep a dead card in two inconsistent states, it is now deleted outright. Everything it did is covered by MT-001 Rampage.

The v1.2, v1.3 and v1.4 notes above are left exactly as written. They are the record of how MT-007 got here and are not retroactively corrected.

Numbering

MT-007 is now a deliberate gap in the melee sequence (MT-001 to MT-006, then MT-008 to MT-013). IDs are not renumbered, so existing character sheets and any other document citing an MT number stay correct.

Appendix: Version History and Designer’s Notes 249

What Changed From v2.0 · Designer’s Notes

Using the Compendium

The Balance Pass. A game master toolkit for the Core System and the Ability Database.

Companion to the Ability Database v1.9 and the Core System. Every enemy gives both a Solo and a Group of 4 Hit Points value, so the same stat block works whether one player or a full party is at the table.

This version is tuned by measurement. Every number below was checked by simulating the fight, roughly a thousand runs per encounter, against a reference party of four (assumptions are in the Designer’s Notes). The design goal is unchanged: a player character should be able to die in almost any fight and avoid dying in almost any fight. Lethality comes from damage that lands hard and fast; survivability comes from information and outs. What v3.0 adds is a ladder you can trust: Routine, Standard, Hard and Deadly now mean what they say.

Reading an Enemy Profile

Every enemy is described in the same way:

  • Identification: the enemy’s name, level, Theme and tags.
  • Description: what the enemy is.
  • Statistics: its Hit Points, Combat Defence, Armour, attack bonus, damage and Wind-Up.
  • Special Abilities: anything it can do beyond the standard rules.
  • Encounter Advice: how best to use it.

Levels. Enemies are grouped by level, from Level 1 to Level 7. Use enemies near your party’s level.

Solo and Group. Each enemy gives a Solo HP value for fighting it alone and a Group HP value for a group of four acting as one.

Tags. Tags say what kind of enemy it is, such as Generalist, Humanoid, Monster or a specialist role.

Shared enemies. An enemy that works in more than one Theme has one definitive profile, in its main Theme. Other Themes list it under their own name and link to that profile.

The Three Universal Rules

These apply to every stat block in this Compendium. They are the balance pass – the tables further down are tuned assuming all three are in play. Adopting the tables without the rules will make the game noticeably more lethal than intended.

Rule 1 – The Damage Floor

A hit always deals at least half the attack’s damage, rounded up, no matter how high the target’s Armour Rating is. Armour Rating is subtracted from incoming damage; the result can never drop below that floor. This is now a Core System rule, along with a cap of 8 on Armour Rating. It is repeated here because every number in this Compendium assumes it.

This applies in both directions – to enemies attacking players, and to players attacking enemies.

Worked example: a Level 7 Rogue Superhuman hits for 17. Against an unarmoured target that is 17. Against a character in Powered Armour with Armour Mastery (AR 6) it is 17 − 6 = 11. Against that same character, a Level 3 Trained Mercenary hitting for 8 would deal 8 − 6 = 2, but the floor is 4, so it deals 4.

Armour is still the best defensive investment in the game – it roughly halves incoming damage at the top end, which is enormous. What it no longer does is delete entire tiers of enemy from the fight. A knife-wielding character can now hurt a Troll, and a Troll can now hurt a knight.

Note: v2.0 of the Compendium carried this rule as a local patch over the Core System’s old treatment of Armour Rating as a block threshold. The Core now includes it, so no override is needed. If your table still runs an older Core, use this rule as written here.this supersedes the Core System’s treatment of Armour Rating as a block threshold, for combat involving enemies from this Compendium. See the designer’s notes at the back – this is the single change here that reaches back into the core rules, and it is deliberate.

Rule 2 – The Wind-Up

Every enemy of Level 4 or higher has a Wind-Up: an attack dealing double its Dmg/Hit. The enemy must spend its Main action on the preceding turn preparing it, and the GM must describe that preparation plainly and out loud.

The Wind-Up resolves at the start of the enemy’s next turn against a target chosen when it was announced. It is defeated by any of the following, all available to a player on their own turn:

  • Moving out of the enemy’s reach, or out of the announced zone.

  • Breaking line of sight – cover, smoke, a Shadow Sphere, a wall of ice.

  • Stunning, Grappling, Restraining, Paralysing or knocking the enemy Prone before its turn comes round.

  • Interposing: an ally moves into the target’s space and takes the hit instead. Deliberate, heroic, and usually a wound.

  • Taking the hit and spending a Wound to reduce it to 0 – always available, always costly.

Wind-Up damage is high enough to drop most characters from full Health in one blow. That is intentional. A party that ignores a telegraph should lose someone; a party that reacts to it should never lose anyone to it. If the Wind-Up target has no possible response – no movement, no cover, no Wound left, nothing – the GM should not have thrown it, and may retarget it.

An enemy that is interrupted does not get the Wind-Up back that round. It has wasted a turn, which is the party’s reward for paying attention.

Rule 3 – Breaking Off

Not every fight has to end in a corpse, and a party that cannot retreat has no way to be smart.

  • Morale – An enemy reduced below a quarter of its Hit Points will attempt to disengage, flee, surrender, or negotiate on its next turn, unless it is mindless, cornered, fanatical, or defending something it cannot abandon. The GM should say which of those applies before the fight starts.

  • The Downed – An enemy does not attack a character who is at 0 Hit Points unless it has a reason to – a grudge, an order, a feeding instinct, or no other target in reach. When an enemy does choose to, the Core System’s 2 Wounds applies in full. Finishing someone off should be a decision the players watch happen, not a dice-roll they lose by default.

  • Withdrawal – A party that withdraws from a zone the enemy is unwilling or unable to leave is out of the fight. Enemies with Grounded, Suppressive Fire, Closing Charge or Bull Rush exist precisely to make that harder, which is what makes those archetypes threatening.

Scaling Rule

Solo HP = Group HP ÷ 4, rounded to the nearest whole number (halves round up).

Combat Defence, Armour Rating, Dmg/Hit and Wind-Up are identical between the Solo and Group versions – a lone monster still only threatens one target per attack regardless of party size. What changes is how many rounds of focus fire it takes to bring it down. Multi-target traits resolve as a single hit against one player.

A few traits buff other monsters and are inert in a solo encounter. Leave them as flavour rather than cutting them, in case the GM ever fields the enemy alongside allies.

Enemy Attacks – the Atk Bonus

An enemy attacks by rolling 1d10 + its Atk against the target’s Combat Defence. As in the Core System, a natural 10 always hits and doubles the damage, and a natural 1 always misses. Atk is the same for every enemy of a given Level, specialists included: use the Atk column of the Generalist Line for the Level of the stat block.

Chance to hit, including the automatic hit on a natural 10 and miss on a natural 1: +8 hits CD 12 / 14 / 16 on 70% / 50% / 30%; +10 hits CD 12 / 14 / 16 on 90% / 70% / 50%; +11 hits CD 12 / 14 / 16 on 90% / 80% / 60%. A well-built defender (CD 15 or higher) is hit a little under half the time; an unarmoured, low-Agility one nearly always.

Setting Flavour: Fantasy and Modern

Every stat block works unchanged in either setting. Only the name and the fictional dressing change: an Armour Rending hit is a warhammer crushing plate in one setting and an armour-piercing round in the other. Each entry gives both.

Threat Budget

The most common way a party dies unfairly is not a bad stat block – it is too many stat blocks. Use this as a starting point for a party of four, then add or remove one Solo statline per player above or below four. ‘Level’ means party level. Outcomes are measured against the reference party in the Designer’s Notes; a weaker or stronger party moves them one row either way.

Encounter What to field Expected outcome
Routine Four Solo statlines at party level − 2 Two to three rounds. Resources barely spent; nobody in danger.
Standard Four Solo statlines at party level About four rounds. Roughly one Wound across the whole party; a character reaching 0 Hit Points is rare (about 1 fight in 50).
Hard Four Solo statlines at party level, plus two at party level − 2 Five to six rounds. Two to four Wounds across the party; roughly 1 fight in 8 has someone reach 0 Hit Points.
Deadly Six Solo statlines at party level About seven rounds. Four to six Wounds; between 1 fight in 4 and 1 in 2 has someone at 0, and a few in a hundred are a total party kill if the party does not adapt.
Boss One Group statline at party level using the Boss Action, plus four Solo statlines at party level − 1 Six to seven and a half rounds. Two to five Wounds; roughly 1 fight in 7 (Level 3) to 1 in 4 (Level 7) has someone at 0.
Do not field More than six Solo statlines at party level, or a Boss with Deadly-sized escorts Not simulated. Beyond what the party’s Wounds can absorb; reserve for scenes the party is meant to flee.

A single enemy at party level is a fair fight against one player and an easy fight against four – the Solo/Group split handles the HP, but not the action economy. Four players get four turns to the enemy’s one. Measured, a lone Group statline at party level fell in about three rounds and cost the party less than one Wound, even with the Boss Action below. Use it as the centrepiece of a Boss encounter, with escorts.

Boss Action. Once per round, at the end of the round, a Group-statline enemy designated as a Boss makes one additional basic attack as a free action, against a different target if it can. It does not combine with a Wind-Up in the same round. This is the cheapest way to give a boss the action economy of a party without adding HP.

The Survival Levers

‘Avoidable if they are careful and smart’ only means something if the players can name what careful and smart actually are. Read this list to your table once. Everything on it already exists in the Core System or the Ability Database – none of it is new.

  • Wounds – Three per character, each reducing one hit to 0 damage. The cost is cumulative: −1 rank on every ability at the first Wound, −2 at the second, −3 at the third. This is the single most important survival resource in the game and the most commonly forgotten. A character at three Wounds is alive, weakened, and should be leaving the fight.

  • Damage interception – Deflecting Dance, Shieldwall, Strider, Absorb Magic, Invulnerability and Ice Constructs all reduce or negate a specific incoming hit. Against a Wind-Up, they are the difference between a Wound and a corpse.

  • Repositioning – Tactical Retreat, Close The Distance, Evasive Action, Flight, Teleportation and Super Speed all let a character leave a zone the moment it becomes dangerous. Zones are coarse – one move usually removes you from a melee threat entirely.

  • Denial – Stun, Commanding Concussion, Freeze Breath, Time Manipulation, Trick Shot and Grappler all stop a Wind-Up dead. Denying an enemy’s turn is worth more than most damage.

  • Recovery – Heal, Improved Heal, Heal Wound, Protection Sphere, Healing Factor and Mutant regeneration. A light rest is only an hour. Wounds specifically cannot be rested off – they need a potion, surgery or Heal Wound, so a party that ignores Wound recovery accumulates a debt that eventually kills someone.

  • Positioning – Fighting adjacent to an ally denies a Skirmisher its bonus. Staying in melee blanks a Harrier. Keeping a Retaliator at range blanks it entirely. Almost every specialist in this Compendium has an off switch, and it is usually positional.

  • Scouting – Foresight, Enhanced Senses, Someone There?, Target and simple asking-around. A party that knows it is walking into a Warden brings weapons instead of spells. Knowing the enemy is the cheapest survival tool in the game.

If a character dies and they could not have pulled any of these levers, the encounter was mis-built rather than well-played. That is the GM’s check, not the player’s.

Building More

To add a specialist at a level not covered above, start from the Generalist line’s numbers for that level and apply one trade-off, rather than stacking a new counter-mechanic on top of full generalist stats.

  • Anti-Armour – Drop HP by 10–15% and Armour by 1–2; raise Dmg/Hit by about 15%; add an armour-ignoring or armour-reducing trait.

  • Anti-Magic – Keep HP at baseline, drop Dmg/Hit by ~10%; add a damage-halving trait against spells plus a mobility trait to close on casters.

  • Anti-Group / Mobility – Drop HP by 15–20%, raise Combat Defence by 1–2 (never above 15); add an isolation-punishing damage bonus plus a reposition-after-attack trait.

  • Anti-Ranged – Keep HP at baseline; add a gap-closing movement trait plus an on-hit effect that pins the target in melee.

  • Anti-Melee – Drop HP by 15–20%, raise Combat Defence by 1–2 (never above 15); add a disengage-on-approach reaction plus a bonus for attacking from range.

  • Controller – Drop Dmg/Hit by 35%; spend the difference on two or more control traits – charm, fear, forced disadvantage, action-stealing.

  • Healer / Support – Drop Dmg/Hit by about 35–45%, keep HP slightly above baseline; add a per-round heal and a per-round shield. Set the heal at one-eighth of the target’s maximum HP and the shield at Dmg/Hit.

  • Reflect / Retaliation – Keep HP at baseline, lower Combat Defence by 1–2 (it wants to be hit) and raise Armour by 1; add a reflect-damage trait plus a once-per-round free counter-attack.

  • Summoner – Drop HP by roughly 25%; add a once-per-encounter summon reusing an existing Generalist’s Solo stat line, plus a payoff for allies dying nearby.

  • Toxin / bypass damage – Drop initial Dmg/Hit by about 60%; add a damage type that explicitly ignores Armour Rating and magic resistance, delivering the rest of its value as an ongoing effect over 2–3 turns that a cure can end.

Keep Solo HP = Group HP ÷ 4, use the Atk of the Generalist at the same Level, and set the Wind-Up at double Dmg/Hit for anything Level 4 or above. For a new setting flavour beyond Fantasy and Modern, swap the two name and description lines – the numbers and traits never need to change.

Specialist Archetypes

Ten lines, each built to counter a specific kind of player build rather than being a bigger Bandit. Each trades power in one place – usually raw HP or damage – for a strong, narrow counter elsewhere, so overall threat stays comparable even though the shape of the fight differs.

A note on using them: a specialist fielded against the build it counters is roughly one difficulty step harder than the budget above suggests. Fielded against a party it has no answer for, it is one step easier. That swing is the point – it is what rewards a party for scouting, and what punishes a GM for picking counters blind.

The ten specialist lines appear as enemies in the catalogues below, at the two levels each is built for.

Special Abilities. None listed. The standard enemy rules in the opening guide apply.

Encounter Advice. Levels 1 to 3 have no Wind-Up. Enemies at these levels kill through weight of numbers and attrition, not single blows.

Armour-Breaker
Level 2
Theme Fantasy (also Modern: Breaching Marksman)
Tags Piercer (Anti-Armour)

Description. A duellist with a war-pick built to punch through plate.

Statistics

Solo HP Group HP Combat Defence Armour Atk Dmg/Hit Wind-Up
8 32 13 1 +8 7 –

Special Abilities

  • Armour Rending – This creature’s attacks ignore the target’s Armour Rating entirely. The Damage Floor is irrelevant against it – full damage always lands.
  • Exploit the Gap – Once per encounter, a hit against a target with Armour Rating 3 or higher deals +4 additional damage.

Encounter Advice. Does not out-tank a heavily armoured player – it goes around the armour instead. Best fielded against a party with a stacked-AR tank; noticeably worse value against a lightly armoured or magic-focused party, since its whole kit is spent bypassing a defence that is not there. Under the Damage Floor its edge is smaller than it was in v1.2 but far more consistent.

Battle Cleric
Level 2
Theme Fantasy (also Modern: Combat Medic)
Tags Warpriest (Healer / Support)

Description. Mends the warband’s wounds mid-fight.

Statistics

Solo HP Group HP Combat Defence Armour Atk Dmg/Hit Wind-Up
10 38 13 2 +8 4 –

Special Abilities

  • Mending Word – Once per round, as a Main action, heal one ally within range for one-eighth of that ally’s maximum Hit Points, rounded up. It cannot heal itself with this. (Scaling the heal to the ally rather than to its own damage keeps a Warpriest worth killing against a Group statline without letting it out-heal a Solo.)
  • Ward – Once per round, as an Interaction, grant one ally a shield equal to this creature’s Dmg/Hit; the next hit against them is reduced by that amount before Health is lost.

Encounter Advice. Deals the least damage of any specialist here – its job is keeping other enemies standing, not hitting hard itself. It forces the party into a real target-priority decision: burn this down first, or let it keep undoing your damage on everything else. Weak whenever a party can burst it down before it gets more than a use or two out of its healing.

Inquisitor
Level 3
Theme Fantasy (also Modern: Suppressor)
Tags Warden (Anti-Magic)

Description. A mage-hunter warded against sorcery.

Statistics

Solo HP Group HP Combat Defence Armour Atk Dmg/Hit Wind-Up
12 48 14 2 +9 7 –

Special Abilities

  • Spellward Hide – Halve all damage taken from Universal, Angelic and Demonic Spells, after Armour Rating and the Damage Floor are applied. Superpowers are not affected – a Warden is no answer to a Voryn.
  • Closing Charge – Once per round, as a free action after moving, this creature may move one additional zone towards the nearest spellcaster it can see.

Encounter Advice. Built to shut down spellcasters: resistant to magic damage and actively closing distance on anyone slinging spells from range. Weak against a party leaning entirely on mundane weapons – its statline assumes it will be eating spell damage, so a purely martial party gets none of that discount.

Cult Enchanter
Level 3
Theme Fantasy (also Modern: Handler)
Tags Controller (Charms, Fear and Disable Effects)

Description. Binds minds with old, patient magic.

Statistics

Solo HP Group HP Combat Defence Armour Atk Dmg/Hit Wind-Up
12 48 14 2 +9 5 –

Special Abilities

  • Ensorcell – Once per round, one enemy this creature can see must make a Presence Roll, DS 14, or become Charmed by it until the start of its next turn.
  • Overwhelm the Mind – On a failed resist against any of this creature’s effects, the target also has Disadvantage on its next roll.

Encounter Advice. Low direct damage in exchange for battlefield-warping control – charming an ally into an enemy, forcing bad rolls, or turning a party member’s own attack against their friends. A fight against a Controller is a fight against the party’s own actions, not a damage race. Weak in a straight damage contest: if its control effects are resisted or cleansed early, it has little to fall back on.

Beastcaller
Level 3
Theme Fantasy (also Modern: Drone Handler)
Tags Summoner (Swarm-Caller)

Description. Calls lesser creatures to swarm the party.

Statistics

Solo HP Group HP Combat Defence Armour Atk Dmg/Hit Wind-Up
8 33 14 1 +9 7 –

Special Abilities

  • Call the Swarm – Once per encounter, as a Main action, summon 2 creatures into the zone using the Solo stat line of any Generalist at least 3 levels lower than this creature.
  • Feed the Swarm – Whenever a creature this trait summoned is destroyed, this creature heals Hit Points equal to half that creature’s Solo HP value, rounded up.

Encounter Advice. Fragile on its own – it wins by changing the shape of the fight, not by hitting hard. Reinforcements pull straight from the Generalist line’s Solo stat blocks, so there are no new numbers to track. Weak once cut off from allies to call in, or if the party can drop it before it gets a summon off.

Duelist
Level 4
Theme Fantasy (also Modern: Free Runner)
Tags Skirmisher (Anti-Group / Mobility)

Description. Quick, lightly armoured, hunts whoever is isolated.

Statistics

Solo HP Group HP Combat Defence Armour Atk Dmg/Hit Wind-Up
12 48 15 1 +9 10 20

Special Abilities

  • Hit and Run – After making a melee attack, this creature may move up to 1 zone without provoking.
  • Pick the Weak Point – Deals +2 damage, +3 at Level 4, against any target that is not adjacent to another ally.
  • Vanish – Once per encounter, become Hidden as a free action immediately after being hit. It may not use this to escape a Wind-Up it has already announced.

Encounter Advice. A fast, fragile duellist that punishes a party for spreading out rather than for what gear it is wearing. Weak in a straight one-on-one slugging match against a single well-defended player – low HP, no armour-piercing or magic-resisting tricks. Its value only shows up against a group of four that lets someone drift away from the pack.

Longbow Skirmisher
Level 4
Theme Fantasy (also Modern: Gunner)
Tags Harrier (Anti-Melee)

Description. Never lets melee fighters get close.

Statistics

Solo HP Group HP Combat Defence Armour Atk Dmg/Hit Wind-Up
12 48 15 1 +9 10 20

Special Abilities

  • Fall Back – When a creature moves into melee range of this creature, it may immediately move up to 1 zone away as a free reaction. Once per round.
  • Covering Shot – Ranged attacks made by this creature against a target that is not adjacent to it deal +2 damage.
  • Suppressive Fire – Once per round, a creature attempting to move into melee range of this creature must first make a Health Roll, DS 14, or lose the rest of its movement for that turn.

Encounter Advice. The mirror image of the Breacher: a ranged specialist that keeps its distance and actively punishes anyone who closes in. Weak against a party built entirely around ranged attacks or spells – it has no answer for pressure it cannot out-kite.

Plague Cultist
Level 4
Theme Fantasy (also Modern: Bio-Agent)
Tags Blightcaller (Toxin)

Description. Spreads corruption with every touch.

Statistics

Solo HP Group HP Combat Defence Armour Atk Dmg/Hit Wind-Up
14 56 13 2 +9 4 8

Special Abilities

  • Toxic Touch – This creature’s attacks deal Poison damage, which ignores Armour Rating and any spell or magic resistance entirely.
  • Creeping Venom – On a hit, the target takes 3 additional damage at the start of each of its next 2 turns, unless it succeeds on a Health Roll, DS 13. Improved Heal, an antidote, or any effect that removes Poison ends it immediately.

Encounter Advice. Deals damage that ignores both Armour Rating and magic resistance – the one specialist that still threatens a party built to shrug off everything else, including a maxed-out armoured tank or a Spellward-proof caster-killer. Its opening hit is weaker than a same-level Generalist; the real damage is in the follow-through, so it is weak against a party that can cure or outheal the ongoing effect early.

Warlord
